ILLUSTRATED FASHIMI.

During the holiday season the everyday use- fulness of the blouse and skirt- costume is more than ever realized, for there is a sense of well- being and comfort in this style of dress which is not to be gainsaid. It also offers .such un- limited scope for many variations of toilette, and in this way appeals to us more particularly when our desires do not coincide with the limita- tions of a dress basket. It is so difficult to get everything one wants packed into a usual sized box when setting out on a few weeks' or a month's sojourn in the country or at the seaside, especially if there are one or two picnics, boating excursions and possibly a few small dances to be expected. Here it is that the possibilities of the blouse and skirt present themselves, for by the aid of a well-cut tweed or serge skirt, to- gether with one or two of a more dressy and diaphanous description, and some pretty blouses, ranging from the morning shirt to the afternoon and evening elaboration of silk, lace, and chiffon, a girl can always look smart and well-dressed. Fashion, too, has always been kind to those who, not possessing a maid, have to do their own packing, for both blouses and skirts this summer are made beparate from their linings. and so are not nearly so difficult to fold smoothly and neatly as they used to be. Tissue paper laid between the folds of the materials and stuffed into the sleeves of blouses or coats pre- vent a great deal of crushing and crumpling which otherwise is unavoidable. The pretty

A PRETTY BLOUSE.

blouse sketched is of printed muslin trimmed with lace and tucked. The design is a very °harming one, and is becoming to any figure. The collarless blouse is indeed pre-eminent this season, with a square-cut lace yoke stopping short at the throat line. This style shows a rounded throat to advantage, but certainly should not be adopted by any one past her first youth, as it is generally on a woman's throat where the first signs of age show themselves. A lace collar should be worn finished at the top by a narrow piping of the same material as the blouse itself. Narrow turnover muslin collars, with a dainty Mock cravat, look well for wear with morning blouses.

Cravats are simple and mostly of the stock kind. The charming blouse illustrateA is made of spotted Bretonne net, with a lace yoke. It is trimmed with Valenciennes lace and insertion, and has a large chou of emerald green satin at one side. The sleeves only reach to a short distance below the elbow, where they terminate

ANOTHER DAINTY BLOUSE.

in a double soft frill of net edged with lace. 01 course a blouse of this design when worn for visiting necessitates the wearing of long gloves of soft suede, which are so becoming to a well- shaped arm. Indeed, it is a pity that the mode for wearing these long ruffled gloves has been neglected of late, as for both summer and winter wear they are comfortable and becoming. Their existence, however, is synonymous with the fashion for wearing elbow sleeves, and as these are now a vogue smiled on by Madame la Mode we shall hope to see more of these dainty articles of dress.

Crystalline is a charming fabric, and, as its name betokens, very light and diaphanous. Another fashionable material for blouses is a fine silky sort of muslin, with a shot or watered look on it. It is termed " Lumineuse." Blouses

are still made plain, but there is an enortaous amount of fine handiwork expended on them, some of the models being so tucked and ribbed and lace encrusted, that little of the original material is left to be seen. Batistes and lawns make up very prettily, and require only a little lace as a trimming. It is, however, an undeniable drawback that so many—indeed I might say all the -nicest blouses fasten down the back. It makes them much more difficult to fasten when one is in a hurry, while they do not keep so flat and in place at the back as those that are buttoned down the front or one side. The last blouse of the trio is designed for evening wear, and is a simple little arrangement

TOE EVENING WEAR.

of silk and lace. It would be found easy .to transform such a blouse into one for afternoon wear by the addition of a yoke of lace arranged .over white chiffon, which is fastened to the figure by tapes from either corner tied round •the waist.

Hairdressing is one of the niceties of appearance to which many girls devote too little attention. The manner of arranging the coiffure is becoming mere and more a matter of individual taste, but a well-dressed head will make any woman look smart, whereas the contrary will ruin the most elaborate toilette. French women have realised the importance of this fact, but not many English women. The style of dressing the hair in a knot en the nape of the neck is certainly more suited to the fashionable hats, and is very becoming ;to some, though not quite se universally as the high eoiffure where the hair is drawn to the top of the head. It does, however, seem more suitable that a woman should display "her glory" to all admiring beholders rather than conceal it under a large picture hat.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Coronation is over at last, and its happy con- summation has brought a general sense of relief, as if a great difficulty bad been overcome. There was a feeling abroad almost approallaing to euperstitious dread that something untoward would happen, and owing to the doubts entertained, it was not until two or three days beforehand that the street decorations were. taken seriously in hand. These and the illuminations were almost, if not quite up to the original programme, and the weather, though dull and overcast, kept fine. There was an entire absence of sunehine which only once more seemed to show bow unfortunate the King has been in the matter of weather on public occasions since his accession. Still, though a little summer brightnes• would have been welcome, it was fortunate that it was not a broiling hot day which would have tried the King, and the distinguished assembly at the Abbey almost beyond endurance. , If only the Coronation could take place in the after- noon, or, better still, the evening, bad long been the cry 'of tbe Peeresses. but it was not to be. For once at least- in their lives, the fortunate recipients of invitations to the Abbey bad to be up at dawn, admit the coiffeur, and don the regulation white gown and robes, not the most becoming toilette at that hour of the day. But if few of tbe Peeresses looked their best, they had the satisfaction of knowing that for once the fact weuld pass unnoticed, all interest being centred upon the King and Queen, the Royal Family, and the ceremony. The King's message to his people on the eve of the Coronation breathed the same spirit which made Queen Victoria's letters atipear as if personally addressed to every one of her subjects. With great charm and grace it expressed his Majesty's heartfelt appreciation of the sympathy that had everywhere been manifested towards him, and his gratitude to Divine Providence that the prayers of the nation for his restoration to health bad been beard. This was the keynote of the message, but it also emphasised the King's view of his life. As originally purposed, there was too much evidence of a desire to simply make it the occasion for junketting and jellification, but the Coronation was celebrated last Saturday in a very different mood, and it is most grati.l tying to know that the King was able to bear the fatigue of the ceremony in the Abbey without any undue strain on his strength. Many well-known people in Society, intend leaving for South Africa later on in the year. Lady Sarah Wilson purposes to return to the scene of her foimer adventures, and also Lady Howe. Some simply seairo to visit the last resting place of those they loved, but many who usually winter abroad, purpose going to South Africa instead of following the beaten track to Egypt, India, and other places. There must be much to see that is of' especial interest—so many scenes I and p`aces made memorable by the war, that it is not I eurprising that " globe trotters " are turning their attention to that part of the world. Moreover those seeking a healthful climate will find all they could desire on the high veldt, and Johannesburg bids fair to become a poptlar resort for people of rank and fashion, during the coining winter. Even the House of Lords is not free from the domestic servant problem, and there seems likely to be a battle royal between the two Houses of Parliament, over the Principal Housemaid of the Upper Chamber. The post carries with it a salary of Ws a week, with apartments and furniture. The Treasury however, seems to have decided that it could not afford the furniture and the Office of Works seized and removed it. Whereupon the Lord Great Chamberlain submitted that this proceeding " was not fair to the housemaid," and a reqeest was made that the furniture should be reltored. Moreover assistance was soueht from the Select Committee on the House of Lord's Offices, who, in the event of the Offic, of Works remaining adamantine on the point have empowered the Lord Great Chamberlain to furnish the Teems himself_ It is a pretty quarrel between the two estates of Parliament, and meanwhile one cannot bele feeling sorry for the housemaid who has been deprived of the use of bee furniture. . .

To make the loose looking rosettes, so much in de- mand for trimming bats for travelling, the seaside, or country, one needs a twelve inch square of panne, soft satin, velvet,or silk. This must be shaped into a circle, and lined with muslin. Then another circle,the size the " pouf " is required to appear when finished, must be cut in stiff muslin,and the large circle gathered en round tie edge. When lightly tacked here and there in the centre, the rosette is ready to fix upon the hat. Black tulle is appearing on much of the daintiest millinery, and is also employed for the airy fairy bow which appears so frequently at the left side of the neck. This latter, for outdoor wear gives a pretty finish to coloured as well as black toilettes.eA more charming hat trimming than black tulle, it would be impossible to imagine. It is sort, cloudy, and becoming, it invests a hat or tcque with a poetic suggestion of mystery. But alas, a shower, or a suggestion of mist, leaves nothing hut a sticky wisp of black, and therefore those women whose dress allowance is limited,would do better to pin I their faith to the finest Brussels net, which, if slightly less effective,is very much more durable. The new travelling capes are being cut longer this year, some indeed reaching almost to the feet. They are not so full as cur old friend the golf cape, and fit well, though loosely to about the waist when they fail into a few folds below the knees. Hoods are con- spicuous by their absence, but no one will deplore this fact, as they are seldom of the slightest tre. The one deep well shaped collar, which is a feature of the best apes, can be turned up, if necessary, when it well pro- tects the neck and face. The fabrics employed are all rough, some exceedingly so, simple strappings of cloth being the only trimmings employed. Many housewives, with the best possible intention to keep their rooms beautiful with flowers, have no more idea of preserving them than that of placing them in a I vase full of water. A little carbonate of soda, however. added to the water will keep them fresh very much longer, as will also a little powdered charcoal, on which the etds of the stalks should be allowed to rest. Faded flowers may also be revived by cutting half an inch off the stem, and plunging the stalk for a moment into boiling water. Of course where one has an urlimited supply of fresh flowers such care is not needed, hut I when they have to bejbought, a good deal may be eave by such little attentions. MADGE.

Tim COLD AUGUST. —A London reporter bas been Informed at the Meteorological Officee that up to now the current month has been the coldest August for 30 years. The maximum temperature on the first ten days has been :—August 1, 66 degrees; August 2, 69 degrees; August 3, 67 degrees ; August 4, 65 degree; August 5. 76 degrees ; August 6, 68 degrees ; August 7;69 degree; August 8, 68 degrees ; August 9. 60 degrees ; and August 10, 64 degrees. In no year within the las: generation did the August temperature fall below tbe? highest temperature recorded in this list—viz.,76 degrees. _ . _ _ _

-The liquidator of the Isle of Man Tramways ap-plied to the Clerk of the Rolls in Chambers on Tuesday for authority to grant to Mr check, the purchaser of th undertaking, a further extension of time for the com- pletion of the purchase, and his Honour extended th, time to the 14th of November. Mr Schenk has paid on account £50,200. The unpaid purchase money, £69,800. bears interest at the rate of five per cent per annum,and the sum paid as deposit is liable to forfeiture in the cas of non-completion of purchase.

OSBORNE HOUSE, ISLE OF WIGHT

WEIDE TEE E.LIG HAS PRESENTED TO FIIE NATION. be devoted to national purposes land converted into a convalescent home for officers of the Navy and Army whose health has been impaired in rendering service to their country. The estate comprises about five thousand acres, reaching far towards Ryde by the sea shore and /to the vicinity of Newport inland. It includes terrace gardens and grounds of surpassing beauty. The sudden death of General Lukas Meyer is regretted by many people in this country who had come to know and respect the gallant Boer leader during his recent stay here. It was only a few days before the fatal attack of heart disease that he left England for the Continent, and then from Brussels came the tidings of his death. The General was Assistant-Commandant General during the War, and was one of the men who, associated with Mr. Schalk Burger, suc- ceeded in fram- ing the terms of settlement which eventuallyproved acceptable to both Briton and Boer, and re- sulted in the longed-for peace. General Meyer was very favour- ably impressed with the beauty and wealth of

agricultural England. He and ricls Mrs. Meyer re. ceived an invita- tion from the

NEI ,EYER.

King to attend the Coronation ceremony in the Abbey. The general replied: "I very much regret to say that we cannot avail ourselves of the invitation. We should dearly have liked to stay, but urgent medical advice compels our going to the Continent Immediately to take the waters." In conclusihn he said they hoped to return to England in October prior to going back to South Africa. It was at the house of an English friend that the general died.

In the reconstruction of the Government no very startling changes have been effected. In some instances the important offices in the Cabinet have not changed hands. Mr. Ritchie, however, as had been anticipated, becomes Chancellor of the Exchequer, whilst the vacancy at the Home Office is filled by Mr. Akers Douglas. [ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.] A QUESTION OF TEXPERANENT BY MARY L. CAMERON.

PART 1.-CHAPTER I.

"Isobel, come and look ! A man has absolutely got down from that rattle-trap old conveyance that goes by the name of the ' Posta '—and more wonderful still, he is going to stop here. They have handed down two portmanteaux and a bag of unmistakably English make ! Don't make a pre- tence of being indifferent, but come quick and look at the top of his head." Catherine Wood was leaning over the balcony of the Albergo Boon' Amico, turning her head back now and then to speak to her friend in the room behind her. _ _ _

"Certainly not! We shall probably see quite too much of him, if not more than we wish to, in the near future, if he is really going to stop here." "How distressingly reasonable you are, Jsobel Considering we have had the Albergo all to our- selves for a fortnight, and considering that I have watched the pasta arrive every day, and that nobody has ever done more than poke a head round the corner of the great leather hood and stare vaguely up and down the flight of broken steps we call a street here—considering and considering—l think you might take a little more interest in a new arrival."

The posta rattled and jangled away, and Catherine came in from her post ot observation and flitted about the great bare sala which ran front back to front of the Albergo, on the first floor, and out of which the bedrooms opened. Isobel Knight sat writing near a window at the further end, which commanded a view of a pine-clad ravine with a torrent boiling in its depths, and beyond it an amphitheatre of rocky peaks. " Why doesn't the padrone bring him up stairs to choose his room?" said Catherine, impatiently. " Perhaps he isn't going to stay here after all," paid Isobel. - "Don't be aggravating! He must stay here, the posta has gone on, and there is no other Alberge in Cima."

At this moment the door leading to the stair-case was thrown open, and the landlord appeared, ushering in a clean-shaven man of middle height, with a mobile, sensitive mouth, and wide-open blue eyes ; he was followed by three or four of the officious loiterers who hang round Italian inn doors, each one bearing a separate article of baggage. "This gentleman is your compatriot. These ladies are also English,', said the host, with the rather embarrassing but well-meant effusiveness of his race. The girls bowed and the new-comer murmured something vague about an unexpected pleasure, and then he was led off across the sala to one of the bedrooms, followed by his ragged regi- ment. " Well, what do you think of him?" asked Catherine, sitting down opposite to Miss Knight. The latter lifted her eyes from her writing, and said:

"I think it is very fortunate that you have a respectable, serious-minded friend here to look after you, that's what I think, Miss Wood." Catherine Wood was the youngest of four sisters, all tall, dazzlingly fair, and reputed clever. Each in turn had gone up to Cambridge and taken degrees with brilliant success, and then the three elder had disappointed all the professors and prin- cipals who had predicted triumphs for them in intellectual fields, by marrying comfortable country squires or prosperous stock-brokers, and lapsing into domestic obscurity. When it came to Catherine's turn she declared, with the want of respect for her elders characteristic of the youngest of a family, that she was not going to throw awa7 the advantages of her education as her sisters had done, and insisted on going straight from college to take up a secretaryship in London. After a year's hard work she fell ill of the influenza, and was ordered off to spend a summer in the mountains to recruit. It so happened that her friend Isobel Knight had just completed her three years' training as a nurse at the London Hospital, and was equally in need of rest and pure air, so the two girls were packed off in charge of one another, with. directions to re-appear fat and well-looking when autumn set in. Leaving England in June, they were well ahead of the crowd of holiday-makers which the last days of July sets loose on the play- grounds of Europe, and wandering southwards, were the very first arrivals at the village on the confines of Italy and the Tyrol, in which the Albergo Buon' Amico was the only hostelry. When Antony Haig returned to the sala after washing off the dust of travelling, he found dinner was laid on the round table in its midst, and that the two young ladies were just taking their seats; his place was laid beside theirs, and by way of beginning the conversation he asked whether they were going to stay much longer at Cima. "We must stay here till we set out on our return journey to England," said Catherine. "The fact is we recklessly spent all our travelling money, except enough to take us home again, during the rst month of our holiday, so we must sit quietly here and economise for the rest of the time. Luckily, Cima is the ideal Alpine village of which we have always dreamed, so we have made our- selves quite happy here, and are having what the Americans call a lovely time." "I hope I shall not interfere with your happi- ness. For I have to wait here for my—for a friend, who is rather an erratic being, and I may have to stay some time. I will try to make myself as little objectionable as possible," said Mr. Haig. "1 daresay we shall manage not to bore each other too much. Perhaps even make common cause and regard new-comers as interlopers, as first arrivals at hotels usually do," answered Catherine. "You make me feel very uncomfortable." "I meant nothing personal. We were rather wishing to be disturbed, weren't we, Isobel ?" "Mr. Haig may be wishing,the same in a day or two," said Isobel, looking up quickly at him and as swiftly withdrawing her glance. So far Antony Haig's attention had been chiefly taken up by Catherine Wood. Her large waist, eolumndike white throat, wavy golden hair, and lon, arms and legs, impressed him as very Greek, and the same time very modern ; sometimes he thought about Clytie as he looked at her, and anon about a heroine of Bjornsen or Meredith. This charmed him; he thought "the proper study of mankind " was woman, • and the more sphinx-like characteristics she had the better. Miss Knight had rather repelled him at first sight ; slight, small, dark women are often brisk and sensible, and Antony hated brisk, sensible girls. Now, however, he surprised something in that quick glance what attracted him. Isobel's big dark brown eyes, so dark as to be called black by most people, had a certain veiled look, an almost imperceptible film, the note of the Oriental eye as compared with the glassy brightness of the southern European. It is common also to the cat and the tiger. In such eyes are possibilities, unpleasant ones often.

"So much the better," thought Antony. "There is nothing to disiover in an amiable woman." _

And he turned to Isobel. "Do you really take me for such a blockhead?" he said, leaning forward and looking very seriously at her. , "I suppose I thought Miss Wood was rather' taking things for granted." "Miss Wood instinctively felt that we should be congenial companions. lam sure she did. Women always feel correctly on such points. It is a gift counterbalancing our slowtkr method of reasoning. You will see that though )you may not like me q better the more you know (I am not such a fep as to presume that), you 11 find that we have subjects and interests in cb mon which will make intercourse interesting, and I hope asreeable to us all."

Isobel looked at him again, as he hopea she would, while he was speaking, but this time her eyes told him nothing, they merely blinked at him like a cat's. Antony turned to Catherine, but she had pulled a book towards her, and resting her elbows on the table, leaned her forehead on the palms of her hands above it, so all he could see was the crown of her head surrounded by an aureole of fluffy fair hair. He felt quite in his element; these girls were not going to give away all their ideas and opinions without an effort on his part which would count as a victory for his tact and insight. "Now between two friends, as between a married pair,",heauffeeted. "there is always one saddled and bridled, and the other booted and spurred. Which is whichrin this case? I wonder how long it wilt take me to,find out."

During the next few days Antony Haig succeeded in making the two girls feel quite at home with him ;.he had a straightforward and at the same time Unobtrusive way of showing that he felt plea. sure in their .society which they could not be ungracious enough to despise. They walked together, _ took their meals together, and in the

evenings discussed all imaginable subjects from the most opposite points of view. Antony had been sauntering about Italy all winter and had a sheaf Of carbon reproductions of the old masters over which they spent many hours of argument and appreciation. Catherine's auda- cious and capricious taste was somewhat of a trial to Antony; he approved a bold rebelliousness in matters ethical or dogmatic, but in art he was strict to severity, and regarded one who would criticise a Botticelli or refuse to bow before a Car- paccio or a John Bellini, as a heretic not even worth faggots to the burning of him. And Catherine not only sometimes refused to bow at the right shrines, but would insist on admiring works whose florid and redundant technique caused Antony a shiver of disgust. He would have been more hopeful about Isobel's ultimate salvation, for she never advanced crude opinions, and listened atten- tively to all expositions, but for her persistence in reading books on art, a weakness from which he vainly tried to wean her. "You should devote yourself at your age to see- ing and judging things yourself, thus cultivating a power of judging soundly, and not confuse ourself with second-hand appreciations," he would ray. "I like to know what other people think about things, and I don't care about torming sound judg- ments," Isobel would` irritatingly reply ; " Miss Wood should please you, she does not trouble her- self about other people's opinions." " I fear Miss Wood's judgments are a little too like prejudices to be quite sound," Antony would reply ingenuously. One wet day he came into the sala from his room where he had been writing letters and found Catherine at her favourite window, watching the humours of the village street, and Isobel reading. "There is a break in the clouds; I believe it will clear up in a few minutes. Do you feel inclined for a walk?"

"I am too lazy," said Catherine. "And I am too industrious" said Isobel, settling herself again to her book. Antony perceived he would have to take his. walk alone, so he started off along the high road, finding that the bye-paths up the mountain sides were converted into running brooks for the moment by the morning's pouring rain. He had not gone very Car when he saw the Posta rolling and rumbling along the road towards him. As it came near his attention was attracted by a passenger who sat on the cross seat behind the driver. He was a lean nervous looking man with a long moustache and hair prematurely sprinkled with grey. The recognition was mutual. . . . . . .

"op'! arreteiz ! feinate !" cried out the stranger to the driver. " Why, Haig ! You here ! This is a piece of luck ! " and he jumped out of the vehicle.

" Where are you staying?" "At Citna, the next village on the road." " I'll walk with you. What is the name of thE inn you are at ?" and after giving directions to thE driver to leave the luggage at the Buon'Amico, tin two men walked on together. ,§ What sort of place is this Cima ? Worth mak• im:f any stay at? I am on the wander for my holiday, stopping wherever I find anything that charms lee.'

"I think you will like it. Undiscovered bF the tourist, it still preserves some local colour and charm—beautiful walks in every direction and— two very interesting girls staying at the Albergo.' "There I recognise my friend Tony. What magnet do you carry about that always brings interesting women around wherever you are ? " "Quite an accidental meeting, I assure you ; I found them in possession on my arrival." "Pure luck, then. Now, wherever I go, the women at the hotels are generally the wrong side of forty and as tough as old turkey hens. It's deuced hard, for my holiday is the only time in the year when I have the time or the chance to cultivate the society of ladies, and I never get the ghost of a look in during it." "Well, stay a while at Cima and share my lusk, as you are pleased to call it." As the two friends returned to the inn, Catherine and Isobel were on the balcony. "Here is Mr. Haig with the man whose port- manteau came out of the Posta,' I presume,' said Catherine.

"The friend for whom he is waiting evidently," added Isobel.

"Which is he, an old young man or a young old one?" wondered Catherine. "His general appear- ance is young, but his hair is quite grey." "He is an old young man, ii you put it that way. He cannot be much over thirty, but something has prematurely aged him," decided Isobel.

THE CORONATION a

The great day which wilt make the year 1902 memorable to all time has come and gone. Edward VII. is a•Crowned King, and the emblem af Sovereignty has also been placed upon the head of his beautiful and beloved Consort. London has witnessed many a moving spectacle, many a stately procession, and many an enthusiastic demonstration of devotion to a monarch, but never a more in- spiriting and impressive scene than that of Satur- day last. Their Majesties' brief progress through the streets of the Capital was marked by an avation of unparalleled exuberance. The cheers of the crowds which lined the streets through which the pageant passed on the way from Buekingham Palace to the Abbey and back again, expressed not only intense loyalty but heartfelt joy and profound gratitude that his Majesty should have been spared to see the glory of a Coronation. Right well the mighty multitude voiced the Empire's gladness, and that the Bing and Queen were deeply touched by this unmistakable proof of delight and love on the part of their devoted subjects we all know. The street pageant, illustrating so effectively the military strength of the Empire, and representative of every portion of the realms over which his

THE KING AND QUEEN IN THE STATE COACH RETURNING FROM WESTMINSTER ABBEY.

Majesty holds sway, no less than the brilliant ceremony within the Abbey, wherein were gathered the highest and noblest in the land, will over recall to memory an event which, to quote the words of the King himself in his gracious message to his people, the Sovereign looks upon as one of the most solemn and important in his life. The procession itself, beyond the presence of their Majesties was such as to evoke the deepest feelings of British pride. The warriors from the South African veldt, associated with bronzed sons of India, and the sons of the Mother Country from Canada, Australia, New Zealand and remote places on the earth, caused such a depth of feeling that cheers could not adequately express. On the ceremony within the venerable Abbey we cannot dwell here from lack of space, but as will be read elsewhere, King Edward and Queen Alexandra were crowned with a majesty of ritual such as is the heritage only of great nations. During the week his Majesty has been almost as active as before hie illness. Early in the week he inspected the whole of the Colonial forces at Buckingham Palace, much to their gratification, for it would have been a bitter disappointment if they had had to return home without having paraded before their Sovereign, and received their medals

A MAN WHO WAS NUMB.

At Sharpness, Gloucestershire, a young man named Phillips, employed in the locomotive department of the Sharpness Dock Company, has undergone a somewhat startling experience, which originated in an insignificant manner. His account of the matter has beee published as follows :

"I first felt a strange numb feeling in my left hand two years ago and then partially lost the use of it. Early this year the same numbness extended to my legs, and yet it W 33 painful to set my feet on the ground. The feeling was horrible, and I could not make out what was the matter until it progressed, and I found I had incipi- ent paralysis. Owing to the lack of control over my limbs, I had to cease work in January, for I would fall down while on duty. A doctor tried a galvanic battery, but I felt no relief. I then went into two hospitals without result, and was discharged as incurable, my hand still useess, and only with great difficulty could I walk."

" But your,appoar to be all right in spite of the doctors' opinions ?" remarked the representative of the " Dorslev Gazette" who investigated the case. s• Yes," answered Mr. Phillips, " but that is due to Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people. My mother persuaded me to take the pills regularly, and after a while—would you believe it P—my arm ac & legs began to regain power, and that deadly numb feeling vanished entirely. You may be sure that I continued the pills, and after perseverance, I was enabled to recommence work, to the great astonishment of all who had heard that I was paralysed and incurable. I fee! as well now as I have ever been, and can do a hard day's work with anybody. Though some of the best medical men were powerless to do me good, Dr. Williams' pink pills gave me back my health." Mr. Bert Phillips is an athletic young man of twenty, and resides at 11, New-buildings Sharpness. Be is an active member of the Union dongregational Church and is held in great esteem.

When such severe cases as this are entirely cured by Dr. Williams' pink pills it is not surprising that they are used with the greatest success in many minor ailments. They have repeatedly cured ansemia, indigestion, biI- iOUSUPSB, eczema, rheumatism sod sciatica, and have restored health in such serious disorders as " decline," consumption, paralysis and locomotor ataxy. But only the genuine pills cure—known always by full title, Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people, on every box. Sold by all dealers, or supplied by the manufacturers, Dr. Williams' medicine company, Holborn-vilduct, London, at two and nine a box, of six kr thirteen and nine, To buy substitutes is waste of time and money.

FISHING FOR A PUMP.

A St. Helens Water Static) n Mishap.

--- Very early in It 19 year Messrs Robinson, Cocks and Co., who had contracted to supply pumping engines and pumps for the two bereholes that had been made at Melling with the object of providing an addition to the St. Helens water supply, lost a huge iron pump down one cf the boreholes, which is 500 feet deep and only 3 feet wide. The pump was worth about £2.00, and the contractors have seven' times attempted to fish it up, but without success, Deep-sea photography failed to afford adequate information as to the position of the pump. Meanwhile, the Corporation became impatient and called for completion of the work. At yesterday's meeting of the Water Committee, Mr Lackland, the engineer, was able to give the welcome news that the pump had been raised, and would shortly be placed in position and the work completed.

CHAPTER II

After the dinner the new-comer, whP had been travelling all the previous night, went 6" his room and Antony irnmediately asked the girl, : "How do you like my friend, Harry Coverclale?" "Very much," said Catherine. "I do not quite make him out," said Isobel. "He is very fresh and simple, as if he came from the depths of the country, and there is something eager and almost timid in his manner to us, show- ing he is very little used to being with ladies, and yet he lives in London. I cannot understand it." "I will tell you about him, and I think you will comprehend. Coverdale was with me at Cam- bridge, he was a very brilliant fellow and a great favourite, and when we left the 'Varsity it was expected that he would Make a name in the world. Then 1 lost sight of him for a bit—l was travelling in Egypt and Greece for a year or two— and when I returned I found he had thrown up everything and was living down in some loathsome slum at the East-end, spending his time in hunting up the children and getting them to school and - starting clubs for working boys and girls. I went down to see him once or twice, but the place depressed me so I really had to give up going there. He has two little rooms in a block of workmen's dwellings, he has made them nice with all his books and a few pictures and so on, but the howling wildernss of passages and staircases and the dreary grimy street outside, and the rags of garments hanging out of the windows, and the blowsy women and hideous shock-headed children and sodden degraded men hanging round the public-houses at every corner! Ugh! How any man of education and refinement can bear to live in it, I can't conceive !"

"Is he a socialist? Oh, I do hope he is a ociabst," cried Catherine, flushing and beaming with eagerness. " Why on earth do you hope that?" "I have so wanted to know a socialist, a real socialist you know who lives up to all his opinions. I have met with lots of drawing-room ones, who have Fabian tracts on their tables and talk either

about collectivism or the perfect life. And at ambridge; London socialists in queer hats and jaeger shirts used to come down to give lectures and say rude things which people never quite knew whether they were to-take as jokes or earnest. But to know a "warranted genuine" socialist personally, has always been the summit of my ambition." "I am afraid Coverdale does not quite fulfil your conditions. He knows and is on good terms with most of the leading socialists as he is with the Ritualist parsons and the Salvation lasses, with everybody in fact who is really working hard amongst the poor down in his slums, but he doesn't call himself by any name. He has just two fixed ideas. One is, that you can't do any good to people unless you understand them, and that to understand then!, you must live amongst them; and the other, that the only way to get at the seething masses of corruption, rotting in our slums, is through the children, that only through them can permanent good be done." "I understand that if a generous-minded man once gets imbrued with the reality of London's misery that he gives up everything else to spend himself in the effort to save something from the wreck of our civilisation," said Isobel. _ "But is

not Mr. Coverdale's sacrffice unoecessarily great i Could he not use his gifts#nd education to influ- nee others lees sensitive, to do the drudgery, who would feel the weight of it less. One can see it i eating his life out, cheerful and full of spirits as he iß.e, "I rather fancy worth tells more, the worse are its surroundings. It is very curious to walk aboun that neighbourhood with Coverdale. He'll tak, you down courts and alleys marked black ir Booth's map, places where policemen go in couples populated by criminals and people who have touched the lowest depths of poverty and deE,,ra• dation add he is not only safe but liked and trusted by these worse than savages. Sore-eyed half-naked children ,get up out of the gutter as he a sses and patter beside him taking hold of hie . finger or his coat tail and looking up in his face, and he is so used to these little attentions that he hardly interrupts his talk, but just gives a smile here and a pat on the head there in recognition ( women run out from dark doorways, creature: with faces like vultures and forms hardly human it their deformity and dirt, and catch him by the gym and drag him aside and pour out their giiiev.

WARRINGTON TOWN COUNCIL.

Coronation Memorial Gift by the Mayor. .

A meeting of the Warrington Town Council was held in the Town Hall on Tuesday evening, the Mayor (Mr J Charltoa Parr) presiding. There were also present :—Alderman Roberta (deputy Mayor). Aldermen Sutton, Monks, Franconab, Shaw Green Councillors Smethurst, Bennett, Ellison, Baird' Barton, Greening, Burton, Roger, William Roberts* James Evans. A J Jolley, A Jolley, Duncan Shillcock, Malkin, John Evans, Tinnion, Sampson. Btough, John Roberts, Hulse, with Mr J Lyon, Whittle (town clerk), Mr Stanley Wilson (deputy town clerk), Mr Fairhurst (borough treasurer), Mr Longdin(borough surveyor) 'Mr. W S Haddock(gas engineer), Dr Gornall (medical officer of health). THE CORONATION OF THE KING.—THE MAYOR'S GIFT. . . .

The MAYOR said, before proceeding with the business of the evening he felt that he must advert to what he was sure was uppermost in their minds as well as in his, and that was the momenteus event of the crow nip of the King and Queen on Saturday last. (Hear, hear). When we looked back to a shott six weeks,tnay even to the las: Council meeting, and contrast the then and now, our hearts were filled with thankfulness to God for His goodness in restoring our King. Then our beloved Sovereign was on a bed of sickness suffering, and he need cot remind them of the sorrow and anxiety which filled every breast, how day by day and hour by hour they scanned the reports of the physicians and doctors, hopine and fearing, and bow the nation, as a nation, bowed the knee to Almighty God in humble supplication for the life it loved se well. And what have we now ?—our King restored to health and crowned with his beautiful and gracious Queen Alexandra, under circumstances and surroundings of grandeur and mast- nificence perhaps unequalled in our annsls, while the voices and hearts of the peep'e of the British Empire in every part of the world throbbed in union with the prayer of" God save the King." (Applause). Ens Wonsan. then read the message of thankfulness from the Bing to his people and said :—lf I may, I should like to take this opportunity of saying what had been in my mind, but which circumstances as you well understand, have prevented me saying before ; that I hope in course uf time to present to the town a memorial of this auspicious event to take the shape of a marble bust of the King—(applause)—to be placed in the Town Bell as a lasting memorial of the Coronation, and as a token of the love, admiration, and thetclevotien of his Majesty's loyal subjects in Warringtoe. (Renewed applause.) THE LATE MR. S. CHANDLEY, The MAYOR said that he regretted to hear of the death of Mr S Chandler. who was at one time a very active member of the Town Council and also mayor o the Borough. He begeed to propose a resolution to the efemt that the Council received with deep regret the announcement of Mr Chandtey's death, and expressed its sincere sympathy and condolence with Mrs Chandley and family in their bereavement, Alderman ROBERTS seconded, and said he asaosia ted himself with all that had been said with regard to the deceased gentleman. THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, AUGUST 15,

WARRINGTON BAND OF HOPE UNION, Gala and Demonstration.

lAlthough forming no part of the local celebra- tions in honour of the Coronation, the gala and de- monstration which took place on Saturday under the auspices of the Warrington Band of Hope Union was a great success,and Coronation day will always be associated with pleasant memories in the minds of those who participated in the proceedings : The assembly of the members of the various bands of hope on the lawn at the Town Hall was watched by a fairly large crowd, many tempsrance workers showing their interest in the proceedings by their attendance. All the organizations were 'well rep- resented, the total number of children and adult', including some of the Rechabites, being about 1,590 A procession was formed on the lawn. the different sections being distinguishable by their banners. There was a sprinkling of flags and bannerettes, and also a good number of temperance mottoes, A very pretty feature was a florally decorated lurry, kindly lent by the Warrington S!ate ComparlY. An archway of flowers had been erected over the centre, under which at two children, suitably Cos- tunaodowlio bore with dignity the parts of King and Queen. A number of other children, representing different nationalities, were in attendance. Headed by the Legh-street Band, the procession moved off promptly at 2.30. At the front were several well-known local temperance workers, namely, Councillors W Roberts, James Evans, L Ellison, Burton, W Brough,and the RevJ S Hughes. The route traversed was Legh-street, Kendriek- street. Tanners'-lane, and through the town to Vic. toria Park. The societies were in the following order :—Friars'-zreen Band of Hope, Ellesmere street Band of Ilepe, Bank Quay Mission Band of Hope Sankey Bridges Band of Hope,Buckley-street Band of Hope. Legh-street Band of Hope, Cock- hedge Band of Hope, Bewsey-road Wesleyan Band of Hope, Wycliffe Band of Hone, Howley Bann of Hope, Brick-street Mission Band of Hope, St Jame!,' Band of hops, Warrington Ragged School Band of Hone. Excellent arrangements lied been made in Victoria Park for the pleasure of the children, who thoroughly enjoyed themselves. The Band of Hope children were provided with an exceMnt tea, tree of charge, and adults were provided with refresh- ments at moderate charges in a tent. The attrac- tions included swings, balloon ascents, cricket and other a Inv,s and a good programme of sperrs with Messrs W Wright, W Lister, and Joseph Owen as judges ; Mr J A Heohin as handicapper, and lr F Podmore alg starter. Letters of snologr for non-attendance were received from the Rev John Yon nd Mr JAHIM Dodds. The CHAIRMAN, in a few introdqttory remarks, sail that so far as the Bend of Hope/movement was con- cerned he was full of hone. If the young cooll he kept sober until they were 21 years of age their opinions on the temperance qirstion would have become matured. There was just one warning he wished to give, and it was this—that the time was coming, and coming quickly too, when not only children, but men aud women would have to have very decided opinimg upon the temper- ance question. Ole great statesman had said that the people of England would have to rise and control the drink traffic, or the drink traffic would control the nation. It would he a sad day for them if the drink did ever control this great nation. (Hear, hear, and applause). The choir sawn the nhorug Merrily Singing " under the conductorship of Mr Spruce. The dating of accom- panist were discharged b Mr F Paimore, _ _ .

The Rev. HERBERT DUNNICO delivered an earnest address. There were two reasons, he said why 69 was a teetotaler and why he opposed to the very utmost the drinking custom of the country. His reasons were first because the drinking custom was the greatest stumbling block to moral good and secondly because it was the greatest stumbling block to all social good. There were priestcraf r,bigotry,and other forms of stumbling blocks, but thee' were nething compared with thestumbling block of drink. They' were assured that 90 per cent of the poverty, disease, and crimo coul I be traced either directly or indirectly to the pernicious habit of drinking intnxicants,and he was told that one-fourth of the popu- lation of London died either in the workhouse hospital or in the lunatic asylum.. These were facts which spoke fer,themselveS, and as long as he was in Warrington an was a Christian minister he was determined to fight uncompromisingly every brewer and every publican as the enemy of God and man. (Flear.hear.) The amount spent on drink averaged £l6 131 41 per family and if that money could be turned into different channels, the question of old age pensions would be settled at once. Dealing with the steps which must 6, taken to oppose drink traffic he said they must all fight for Sunday clos- ing and the enforcement of the licensing laws already in existenoe. He pointed out that it was as criminal to set ve a drunken man as it was to get drunk, but whilst them mere 36,000 eonvictions for drunkenness last year only 177 publicans were fined. Mrs. ROBERTS then distributed the prizes, after which Miss Carrie Hastings very sweetly sang the wcll-known song" Daddy." The chortle was then sting" Hurrah I for the men in the gap." Councillor Woe ROBERTS Said they had had a verr happy day and he was glad the temperance boys and girls of Warrington had had such a happy day on the Coronation of the King and Queen. (Hear, hear.) He had been asked to move a very comprehensive vote of thanks to Mrs Roberts for distributing the prizes, to Ccuncillor Evans for presiding, to Mr Minnie° for his excellent address, and to the committee and children for their services. He was glad to find that they had in Mr DellThiCO a new champion of the temperance clause, and he hoped they would all rally round him and sup- port him in his work. They were not as badly off as some people thought in ther temperance work. They had not made much noise in Warriagton lately, but he assured the meeting that they were silently progressing. Some of them beloned to that terrible body the Warringtoa Town Council. But it was not quite as bad as it was painted ; still it was bad enough. Out of 36 members ten were total abstainers. (Applause.) They had actually more total abstainers in the Council than publicans, and he hoped the numbers would gradually increase until the whole 36 belonged to the Warrington Band of Hope Union. (Hear, hear, laughter and applause.) He was pleased to see five members take part in the procession that day. (Hear, hear.) In conclusion, the speaker urged the , parents present to encourage their children to take an interest in band of hope work. (Applause.) ' Mr TRomas DALE seconded the motion, which was heartily agreed to, after which cheers for the King and Queen and the Mayor and Mayoress were given,bringing this part of the day's proceedings to a close. The games in the field were then resumed and continued until dusk.

1902.-

CORONATION CELEBRA- TIONS AT WARRINGTON. Interesting Proceedings.

Warrington wore a holiday aspect on Saturday on the occasion of the conflation of King dward yli and Queen Alexandra. There was a moderate display of flags and bunting and work was almost entirely suspended. The chief feature of the cele- brations was, of course, the public procession, which was postponed in June last owing to the King's illness. In many respects, the procession was similar to those which took place in the town to celebrate the coronation of Queen Victoria in 1833, of William IV in 1831 and of GeorgeTV in 1821. The Walking of "the clubs" as they were then called, tormed part of those proceedings and Saturday's procession was largely composed of members of the local friendly societies. Their striking banners and regalia added a touch of brightnees and colour to the proceedings which left a pleasing impression on the minds of the spectators who gathered along the route of the procession. The and members of the societies were conveyed' in open landaus and there was at least one venerable member, Brother Marsh, whose recollections went back to the celebrations in honour of the Coronation_ of George IV.

THE PROCESSION.

Far this important function, elaborate arrange- ments bad been made by the Town's Marshal, Mr Luke Talbot, and a procession committee composed Of the leaders of the .rlendly societies. The whole ,of the proceedings passed off without a hitch and this happy result reflected great credit on all who were responsible for the arrangements. The Com- ponent parts of the Procession assembled in front of the Town Hall and at 10 30 when the procession was ready to start, Bugler Slater, from Orford Bar- racks sounded the "advance." The Mayoress then waved the National flag which was the signal for the Volunteer Regimental Rani to play one versa of the National Anthem. This having bien does, the pro- cession immediately moved off, arid proceeded to pass out of the Town Hall grounds by the centre gate. The following was the order of procession :- 1 Detachment of Police. 2 VOlunteer Band and Battalion. 3 Ambulance Brigade. 4 Fire Brigade with engines, 5 Clergymen of all denominations. 6 The Mayor, mayoral suite, and Borough Member. 7 Freemen of the Borough. 8 Aldermen, Councillors and Justices of the Borough. 9 Principal Corporation officials. 10 Guardians of the Poor and Rural District , Councillers. 11. Friendly Societies.

Vie detachment of polica had at their head P C's Howard and Gibbs, both of whom have recently re- turned from South Africa. They wore the khaki uniform of the Grenadier Guards, to which they belong, aad Gibbs carried the town marshal's flag. There was a representative muster of Volunteers, about 300 being present. They were headed by two pioneers. Next came the regimental and bugle bands, under the direction respectively of band- masters Richardson and Plinston. The Mounted Infantry company and the cyclist section formed a part of the procession. In the absence of Colonel Ridgway the whole battalion was under the com- mand of Captain' Appleton. Lieutenant Realm, acting adjatant, was in charge of the cyclist com- pany, and the only other officer present was Lieut. Fulton Smith.

The Ambulance Brigade,about twenty in number. carried a littss and stretcher, and were under the command of the Chief Superintendent (Captain Waring). Sept Baldry, and Mr T J Hulse, one of the officers and hon secretary of the corps. The -members of he Fire Brigade formed an 1 attractive part of the Precession. They had with them the steam engines " Captain " and " Major " and the old manual en.rine " Nile." The funnels of the two first-named engines were filled in with flowers and evergreens, and other parts were simi- larly decorated, the effect producel being very pleasing. Superintendent Turner was in command, and he was accompanied by 'his son, the Deputy Superintendent, and 16 other members of the brigade.

The clergy and ministers of all denominations were represented by the Revs J L Milne (Presby- terian), T Bishop, C H Hocken. and W C J Neville (Wesleyan), J S,Hughee and H Dunnico (Baptist),; Reveif C B Carleton, A F White, G W Dickenson, TI RlFild, A Inglis, w Bracecamp, J H G Holling- worth, B Percival, D Kerrin (Church of England), and Fathers Whittle, Wilson, Mercer, Noblett, and Haulms (Roman Catholic). Preceding them ea faoe. were the sword bearer (Sergeant Dean) and the mace bearer (Sergeant Williamson who acted in the absence of Sergeant Bell). It had been arranged that the member for the Borough (Mr R. Pierpoint) should accompany the Mayor but be was unable to be present. A Camarta, WOMAN.—" Speaking of women with saving dispositions," said Dixmyth, "my wife's in a class all by herself." "How so P' queried his friend Hojax. "Last week I bought an upright piano," replied Dixinyth, "and my wife made a beautiful green plush cover for it, so the polish wouldn't get scratched. Yesterday she made another cover of linen to go over the plush to prevent that from getting soiled. Next week I suppose she'll make a calico cover to protect the linen. Oh, I tell you women have great,_ fertile minds." "DARLING," said the young man, as he tenderly raised the lovely, head from the place where it had rested an hour or more, got up, sat down again on the other side of the beautiful maiden, and once more drew the golden curls to his bosom, "von have no 'objection to this slight change of position, have you ? " " No, Alfred," she murmured softly, "your heart is on this side." "Yes, my angel," responded the young Man, his voice trembling with deep feeling, "and my vest will now get worn alike on both sides." " WE'LL have to give up the idea of louttin pictures in the parlour, Jane," remarked Old John Turnipseed, as he threw the bridle under the table. " Why ?" asked his wife. "Too dear! Why, I priced one in town to-day, and the dealer sez, sez he That's an old' master; its price is £.500.' " ' Why,' sez I, it looks like a secondhand pictur.' " Yes, it is,' sez he. "Then, thinks I, if a second hand pictur costs that much, it's no use to price a new un. So Jane, I reckon we'll have to hang up a few mottoes, and the like, and let tie pictur a go."

THE KING'S recovery hee astonished even the most hopeful. It Wile generally believed that, even if he were able to go through the Coronation ceremony at all, it would only be under such conditions as would deprive the ceremony of most of its splendour. But the .g 154 has not only been crowned—he has been crowned in wonderfully better health than anyone predict' ed for him, and he is none the worse for the strain, , It is a very remarkable and gratifying achieve' ment. No doubt one chief factor in the KINGS restoration has been his own fortitude. It v 759 , resolved, at all costs to his comfort, to prevent repetition of the bitter disappointment of lest June, and his country owes him much gratitucle for his courage and perseverance. The KING bo' always attached great importance to demonstrations as belong to all parties common. He has always shewn that be rellises that the crown is the symbol of the " common dignity, and sympathies of 13164 countrymen. Everyone recalls how prompt 13 was to join in the general tribute to Mr GLAD' ' STONE'S memory at the funeral of that great stater ° man. Saturday's ceremony was not the cornmeal° ation of any ideas of conquest or plunder or race pride. It was a ceremony which is much older than those ideas. The Colonies who joined in 15 represent not the modern spirit of Imperialism,bo the old traditional ideas and forces of free govero- ment. The KING was not crowned as a conqueror but as the ruler of free men; and his Coronatio° ought to recall men's minds to the great truth that the most splendid things in British histell' in which that ceremony now finds its place, have not been war and dominion, but freedom and self- respecting power.

IThe Coronation.

The nei/ peers and knights and so forth received Wit favours early because his Majesty knew that in bis precarious condition of health there was a danger, that he might not otherwise be able to aw'3ol these distinctions. But the intention to remeer. ber the people, and to do so in such a muniliceo manner as by the gift of Osborne House for evert was kept secret until his Majesty had sat in tb. chair of ST. Pe, certainly had every indication of their regard fe'" him and their enthusiasm on his recovery t'ci health. The KING'S thoughtful act is the 0014 noteworthy because, with the enlargement of tile Privy Purse made on his Majesty's accession °at the throne, the maintenance of the estate a 'Osborne would not have been a burden to hil3l; Windsor and Balmoral undoubtedly involve heavy expense, and residence at Buckinghet.° , Palace for part of the year will be costly enoug'" but the nation has provided his Majesty with e° ample income ; and,despite all rumours to the CO trary, the Sovereign is not extravagant 10 tastes if we allovv for the demands which! exalted station makes upon hira and his l'lghe sense of correctness. And his gentleness. To shew the latter without the first named would be to play the woman. To take an interest only in the vigour of arms, whether in scarlet or khaki,would be to ignore the essentially domestic nature of English life. King EDWARD, in his concern for our warriors and our hospitals, touches the popular sentiment, and all the more warmly because we know that those feel- ings are perfectly sincere. To the officers in the two Services the provision will come as a great boon. We kno s how the private incomes of our county 'families have shrunk, and how difficult it is for them to help their sons who havo commissions in the Army. We know it is still harder for men who have risen from the ranks to meet the exi- gencies of their higher station ; and many of them have nowhere to go when they are discharged from hospital as convalescent. Daring the South African war much was done by private benevo- lence, and the Misses KEYSER in particular set a splendid example by devoting their residence in Grosvenor-Place to the care of the officers who, brought home from the front, still required nurs- ing. The KING made frequent visits to this Home of Rest, and the ladies and the nurses had the pleasure of accumulating a long row of photo- graphs of distinguished officers who recovered health under their care. These included many of the Victoria Cross winners. But private benevo- lence, however delicately provided in the name of hospitality, does not properly meet such cases. Osborne House, with the gentle air of_ the Solent, can be made to relieve much anxiety, and both the nation and Army will be deeply grateful to the KING for this act of mueificence, and will cherish in association with it the memory of good QUEEN VICTORIA.

IT was thought at The Education Bill, one time that the fierce discussions on the Educa- tion Bill would be prolonged beyond the Corona- tion. This expectation was not fulfilled, but Mr. 'BALFOUR is greatly mistaken if he thinks the majority in the Commons has settled the fate of the Bill. The TIMM of Commons has so far de- cided two things of great importance. The first is that the School Boards are to be abolished; the second that the public authorities are to bear only a one-third representationnon the governing body of the Voluntary Schools. But this decision is not final, for no one can pretend that the Government has ever taken the country into their confidence. There will be a fierce and strenuous agitation in the country against the Bill. In the House of Commons it is not only Liberals that resent the injustice of the new arrangement respecting Voluntary Schools. Several Conservatives and Unionists, including Sir MICHAEL FOSTER, Mr. MIDDLEMORE, and Mr. TRITTON, have all protested strongly against the unfair distribu- tion of private and public control in the govern- ment of schools that are to live on public money. Mr. LLovo-Gironas put the policy of the Govern- ment in a happy phrase when he said that the Government were on the rocks and they were putting the chaplain on the bridge. Mr. BALFOUR is possessed by the belief that the parson must be left to control the schools. Several members of his party have suggested that it was possible to make all facilities for giving definite religious in- struction in the schools and yet allow public con- trol. Arrangements of this kind are in operation in many of the colonies. But Mr. BA.LFOUR will not hear of it. He is satified with nothing short

43f a system that gives the Voluntary achool party the full control; the teachers must be appointed, and not merely the religious instruction given, by the denomi- nation. Mr. BALFOUR is quite careless of the great hardship this plan imposes on the Noncon- formists and the public. It is a plan the country will not accept. If the Church only asks for some arrangement by which denominational teaching an be given the demand can be met. It is met in Szotland. It is met in other countries. But the Church demands that the State be allowed to control all these schools, towards which she con- tributes nothing but part of the cost of the build- ing and the upkeep. The Church is to have the use of these schools on Sundays,and yet the public is to pay for thcir maintenance without the right to their control. Mr. BALFOUR is acting very foolishly in persuading himself that this kind of plan can be forced on the country. ft will be fiercely contested, and the result will be angry feuds and the further embarrassment of a great public interest.

-- When England was defeated at Old Trafford in the fourth test match by three runs we were quite sure that we had seen the most exciting test , match ever played and almost as confident that there would never be another to equal it for ' sensation. Yet the very next, and last, match has surpassed even this memorable encounter in all respects. First and foremost England won and -saved herself from complete humiliation at the , hands of the clever Australians. The victory, too, , was the narrowest possible, for the last batsmen • came in when 15 runs were wanted--seven more than at Old Trafford—and,thank the fates and the . plucky Yorkshiremen ! they were in to the end of the chapter which closed with an English one wicket victory. Hirst and Rhodes well deserved , the ecstatic encomiums lavished upon them, as did their fellow Tyke the Hon. F. S. Jackson,but there was a greater even than they. Gilbert Jessop came in when five of England's best wickets had ' . fallen for 48 runs and there were still over 200 re- quired. Was he dismayed? Not he! He set about , the bowling as only he knows how and by a dis- play of simply electrifying cricket rattled up 101 runs in 70 minutes. The incomparable Trumble and the insidious Saunders were treated with a , lack of ceremony unheard of the latter be even being n ' " knocked-off "by Jessop's cyclonic hitting. He undoubtedly won the match, not only by scoring the runs he did but by getting them in such quick L time, for rain came on immediately after the finish. It was a Wonderful match, and now that the battle is over we can look back with some satisfaction for, though our visitors have won the rubber, who shall say they are superior to our- , , selves ? The old country is not played out yet and we shall have those "ashes" when once Dame -Fortune deins to smile. . 6000.11.1=1.•••••••••••••••••••••1....1.1.1.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

CORONATION TWINS.

Them was a special distinction conferred upon the household of the Rev J Osborne, pastor of the Runcorn Bethesda CJngregational Church. on Coronation Day. The event, upon which Mr Osborne has received the congratulations of his numerous friends, was the arrival of twin girls. The mother and daughters, we are pleased to say, are doing well. NARROW ESCAPE FROM LDROWNING.

On Tuesday afternoon Attred Haddock, aged 7 years. of Surrey-street., Runcorn, had a narrow escape from drowning in the Bridgewater Canal at Top Locks. A number of youngsters were fishing and playing on the banks of the canal when Haddock fell into the water and had sunk three times before a workman named C Az•hcroft, who had been attracted by the boy's cries for help, came on the scene and fished him out with a boathook. COWARDLY ASSAULT AT WIDNES.

On Thursday, at Widnes, John and Joseph McMahon, Ditton, were summoned by Richard Hall, Runcorn, for as3ault. Complainant stated that on the night of August 6th the defendants attacked him in Ditton-road without any provocation what- ever. A constable deposed to finding Hall lying across the footpath bleeding from the head and face. The Bench characterised the assault as a cowardly one, and fined each defendant lOs and costs or 14 days. CAMDEN WESLEYAN CHAPEL, RUNCORN.

The Nelson-street We4leyan Sunday School anni- versary services were held in the Camden Chapel on Sunday, when sermons were preached morning and evening by the Rev J A B Malvern, formerly of the Runcorn circuit. In the afternoon Mr Malvern delivered an address to parents and children. Mr M Frith presided. Special hymns were rendered by the choir and scholars. Collections were taken at all the services:in aid of the Nelson-street schools.

SHIP CANAL LIGHTS AS FATALITIES AT RUNCORN.

On Tuesday, at Runcorn, Mr It Dobson, coroner, held an inquest on the body of Joseph Yates, aged twenty-five years, of Cawdor-street, Runcorn, fire- man on the Ship Canal tug Mercia, who was drowned in the canal on Friday night.—moses Kenwright, 7 Heath-road, fireman on the tug Agnes Seed, deposed to deceased and himself having two glasses of stout in the Boathouse Inn shortly before eleven o'clock on Friday night. They were both sober and went together into the Old Quay engineer- ing yard. Their vessels were moored in the vicinity It was a very dark night, there were no lights, and deceased would have to cross two awkward places to reach his ship.—The jury returned an open ver- dict, and suggested to the Manchester Ship Canal Company that the approaches to the quays should be lighted, especially on dark nights. _ EIUDDEN DEATH OF A RUNCORN WOMAN.

Mr E A White, deputy coroner, conducted an inquiry at the Runcorn Police Station, on Monday afternoon, into the circumstances attending the sudden death of Sarah Ashbrooke (66), a widow, who lived at 26 Okell-street, Runcorn.—William Ashbrooke, son of deceased, said his mother had a stroke about two years ago, and had been ailing ever since. During the last week or so she had com- plained of pains in the chest. She was ill on Thurs- day, and she went to bed about half-past eight. Witness came downstairs at nine o'clock on Friday morning, and found her lying on the kitchen floor. She was partially dressed. He tried to lift her,and found that she was dead. He brought a neighbour in, and sent for the doctor.—Dr Bethune said he saw the deceased at noon. She had been dead less than twelve hours. It was probable from her ap- pearance and the evidence of the son that death was due to failure of the heart's actiou, brought on by old age and general debility.—A verdict in accordance with the medical testimony was re- turned.

STRANGE MANNER OF RETURNING GRATITUDE. Before the Llangollen justices. on Thursday, a Runcorn labourer named Thomas Massey,alias John Lowe,was charged with unlawfully assaulting Miss Eliza Roberts, a respectable lady, residing near Chick. According to the police evidence, prisoner, who was proceeding to south Wales, admitted he had indulged freely in drink, and called for refresh- ments at the house of the prosecutrix last Sunday. Massey was hospitably received and Miss Roberts engaged him to procure some water from an adjoining well. She accompanied him, both returning to her house, where she sup- plied him with free refreshments. He ultimately assaulted her She obtained a warrant for his apprehension, and the Chirk and Vroncysyllt police succeeded in tracing prisoner to Oswestry on Tues- day night, when he was brought in custody to Llan- gollen. The case caused great amiLsernent, prisoner declaring the beer proffered him was sour. He simply thanked her by placing his arm around her. The Bench, in sentencing the prisoner to seven days' imprisonment, considered the prisoner's action a strange manner of returning gratitude.

KILLED IN THE STREET AT I

RUNCORN•

Mr Dobson, coroner, on Tuesday, at Runcorn, held an inquest upon the body of Emily Elizabeth Sant- ley, aged nine years, who was killed in Egerton- street, by a mineral water lurry on Saturday after- noon.—Joseph Santley, flatman, of 5, Egerton- street, identified the body, and said some years ago his daughter was run over by a milk earl. She could hear quite well, but could not talk properly. He was not aware that she ran after carts.—Ernma Young, 34, Wellington-street, said she saw deceased lying on the ground. Shingler, the driver of the cart, went to her and placed his arm beneath her head. Deceased was bleeding from the nose and mouth. She thought the child was dead. Witness asked Shingler what be had done and he replied that he did not know. The , cart had passed the deceased but he did not know whether the cart had passed over her. The child was on the right hand side of the cart. John J Shingler carter, Main-street, Frodsham, said he was employed by Messrs Ellison and Corker,mineral water manufacturers, Frodsham On Saturday he came to Runcorn with a lurry load. He was lead- ing his horse from the George and Dragon and turning the corner of Wellington-street. from Egerton-street, when an old man shouted "Did you see that child run against your waggon"? He , stopped the horse and ran and picked up the child; who died in his arms, He asked the old man how it was done, and he said the child '* ran smack against the waggon." Witness did not know how the accident happened but he did not think the wheel went over the child. There was no mark upon the wheels.—The Coroner said his own idea was that the wheel did pass over the child and that her head was smashed. There was no doubt the child was killed by the cart, bui, the evidence was not satisfactory. It seemed to him perfectly clear that there was no carelessness on the part of the d river.—The jury returned a verdict that the child was accidentally killed and absolved the driver of the vehicle from any blame.

LICENSING CHANGES AT NEW-T-3N. The magistrates sitting at Newton Petty Sessions on Thursday—Messrs T j Gillespie, IL Stone,Colonel Wood, and W Collingwood—granted the following transfers :—Oak Tree Inn, Newton, from Louisa M Toft to Jimes Marry Toft ; Joiners' Arms,Croft,from the late Samuel Deacon, to Martha Jane Dascon ; Fiddle-F-th-Bag, Burtonwood. from Elizabeth Nicholls to Matthew Pimblett Pownall.

RUNCORN NOTES

LBY A CORRESPONDENT].

Last Saturday the Frienaly Societies held the demonstration they should have held some weeks ago in commemoration of the Coronation of the King. The turn-out was a very imposing one, and a semi-official air was imparted to the proceedings by the Societies being headed by the Cuief Citizen of the town and the City Fathers. It cannot be said, however, that Coronation Day was observed as a general holiday. While many tradesmen shut for a few hours, still fewer closed all day, the majority remaining open the whole of the time. A minority made a pretence at decorations but, gener- ally, the inhabitants were content with what they had done some little time ago. Altogether Runcorn did not commemorate in any particular marked manner the crowning of His Majesty King Edward the VII. on Saturday last.

The banquet in commemoration of the King's Coronation given by Mr Taylor .1.P., (.chairman of the Urban District Council), Mr Knowles (chairman of the Rural District Council), and Mr Stokes (chairman of the Board of Guardians), was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday. some of the speeches were of a very eloquent order indeed and , one could not help feeling impressed with the good ' feeling which prevailed among the members of the different authorities. Toe most pleasing feature of the gathering was the presentation to ir Moseley- Williams on the occasion of his marriage.. It is interesting to record the fact that the gentleman has turned the corner of the Psalmist's three score years and ten. For a very long time Me Williams has represented Frodsham and has done yeoman service to the Board and the Council. In a few months the probability is that he will sever his connection with the authorities with which he has so long been associated. As was justly remarked at the meeting his removal will be a loss to the Board and to the district generally. The chairman of the Urban District Council was in a jocular but nevertheless practical mood. In a very fine out- spoken utterance Mr faylor avowed that the reason why he so continuously attended the Board meet- ings was due to the fact that he did so out of pure love for the poor classes with which the Board had to:deal. Such a noble sentiment was applauded as it deserved to be to the very echo. It is to be hoped that the people of Runcorn take note of these human touches which make the whole worli kin and appreciate them as they ought to be.

The Thelwall nuisance still exercises the soul of Admiral Jones-Parry. On Tuesday he was on the war-path and it took the Medical Officer all his time to answer the very pertinent questions put by him. Without a doubt the Admiral is entirely right in his contentions. There can be positively no justification why certain sewerage works at Thel- wall should have been stopped for over a month the inhabitants meantime suffering the greatest incon- venience in constquence of the effluvia which arises from the semi-completed works. It is also only fitting and proper that Mr Naylor should have the report that as wks for. Ana it further is abso- lutely necessary that the contradictory reports on the state of the Thelwall water should be reconciled by those whose duty it is ta reconcile such state- ments. The Council are not yet out of the wood by any means. The majority think they are but there will be a rude awakening, it is to be feared, ere long.

The inmates at the workhouse are going to have a lively time of it before the harvesting season is over. The Guardians—noted tight-fisted custodians of the ratepayers' purse—are determined that the able-bodied men who are fit to do a day's work shall not eat the bread of idleness. Those who can help to swell the song of harvest home are to be ap- proached and offered a fair day's wage for a fair day's toil. The probability is that not many will feel disposed to accept the Guardians' invitation. If the desire to shirk work is positively shown the result which must necesarily follow .will be entirely justifiable and the would-be idlers ban expect but little sympathy. At all events the Board are acting wisely and economically in endeavouring to lessen the numbers in the house and so save the ratepayers' pockets.

SUDDEN DEATH OF CAPTAIN H.

R. D'A. WILLIS.

Captain H. R. D'A.. D C.. of Halsnead, Whieron, near Preset, and brother of the Lim:, Canon Willis, rect3r of Warringtoo, was on Wednesday after- noon found dead in the train from Wigan which arrived at Edge-hill at 440 p.m. The ticket collector upon opening the door of a first-class compartment of the train discovered the body, which was taken to Lime- street Station, and thence conveyed, under the direction of a Police-constable, to the Prince's Dock Mortuary. Captain Willis was well known to the station officials, who communicated the sad news to Mrs Willis. The deceased was one of the chief mouroers at the funeral of the late Lord Gerard, at Garswood, on the 2nd inet, and on his return home be met with a nasty accident at Elaydock. His horses were startled by an electric tram, with the result that his carriage and the car came into violent collision. Captain Willis and the coachman were thrown out, and Captain Willis was considerably bruised. It is surmised that the shock he then Buff -trod mav have had some connnetion with his sudden demise.

Mr Henry Rudolph D'Anyers Willie was a cdunty justice for a great number of years, also a deputy lieu- tenant of the cpuncy. He was formerly a captain in the Army, but he retired over thirty years ago, when be succeeded his uncle in the Halsnead estate at Whis- ton, near Presoot. On two occasions he has made large additions to the estate, which is of very onsiderable extent. He leaves a widow and two daughters, and his son, Mr Richard A D'A Willis, is at present in India
