VIADUCT INSTITUTE,EARLESTOWN.

Annual Meeting. The annual general meeting of the members of the above Institute was held in the large reading room on Wednesday evening. tr J W Emmett pre- sided,but there was not a large attendance ef mem- bers. The first business was the reading of the elecretary's ()Jr T Ormand) report which stated That in reviewing the income and expenditure for the 12 months to the 30. h June lait, we find there are so very tear items differing from those of the previous year that our reference to the accounts-is fortune' ely necessarilybrief.The total income for the year is £387 93 51 or £4 11851 less than for 1901, which is more than accounted for by a decrease of nearly £7 in visitors' fees. Taere are several con- tributory causes, the chief of which is one so oftenl taken advantage of, the weather. We have had 1 more than the average number of wet Saturdays whicb,a3 you know,militetee against "good gates.' The same reason may be assigned for the receipts] from bowling being still below those of a few yearsl ago, although they show en improvement of XI Is 24d compared with last year. The com- mittee are pleased to record a slight increase in the number of entries for the two bowling handicaps-201 as compared with 179. The total amount expended in actual maintenance is £351 15.5. You will, however, observe under the head " Library Account" that £2O has been set aside for preparing, printing and issuing a supple- mentary catalogue of books added to the library from November 1894 to date. This catalogue we hope will be ready for distribution in the course of a few w .eks, hence our omission in the statement of accounts, of a list of those books purchased dur- ing the year just closed. In the perk account £5 has been transferred te the reserve fund for asphal- ting, &c. There is also, in the same section of expenditure, a new item of £5 to the gardeners Your committee unanimously agreed a year ago to pay the gardeners at the close of each season, £5, that is to say, £2 to the head man, an i lOs each to the or her two, if the grounds and everything ap- pertaining thereto were kept In a first class condi- tion. In the geueral account there are two special grants of 10 guineas each. One to the Newton- in-elak-rfield Lectures Committee to Yards the cost, of lectures, and one to- the Earlestown Prize kihnd. Tbe newspapers and periodicals have cost .ErS 183 ed as against £B5 103 6d for 1901. During the year we have added to the news-room the following magazines, viz., " L)comotive," "Fieldens "and "the Railway." The weekly papers have been increased by "Invention," and " the Cyders' News," and" Motor Cycle." We have ceased taking "the Speaker' and have reduced the number of copies of the early edition of the Man- chester "Evening News" frog ten to seven. In several previous reports, excepting the last, oar criticism on tae attitude of the band has not been very favourable. It must, however, be obvious to all of yoe that any such strictures this year would not only be out ot place, but unjust, seeing that the members of the band have, on three separate even- ings this summer, rendered a number of beautiful selections in the park, to the delight and admira- tion of all those who were able to be there It is not perhaps generally known that the admis- sion to the grounds any week-night after six o'clock is only one penny, to those who are not bona fide members. Those of you weo compare one year's statement of accounts with another will see that £1 13e only has been realised by the sale of Park tickets, the same as for 1901. We would again emphasise the fact that these tickets are available from the Ist April to the 30th September, and are issued to non-members at a charge of 23 6a for the season. Any of the Company's workmen's sons, under the age of 18, employed at any place in the district, other than the Viaduct Works, can have these Park tickets at Is for the season. (Application to be made t 3 the Librarian.) A member's full ticket, entitling him to every bene- fit the Institute offers, and there are many, is 2s per quarter. It does not appear to be generally known that Park tickets are ,ssued to the widows of I deceased workmen, free of charge, on :application to the Secretary. During the year ended the 30th June lest, 107 new books have been added to the Library, at a cost of £23 Os 2d. The number of readers during the year just ended is 336, as compared with 212 for 1901, to whom 9089 volumes are issued, an increase of 1351 or 17i per cent. Your Committee have noticed with keen regret that for a number of years now the annual meetings have been very sparsely attended. Your Committee are of opinion that if the Institute year ended on the 30th September instead of the 30th June, and the annual meeting be fixed for either October or the early part of November, it would in very many respects, be beneficial and much more conducive to the well-being of the Institute gener- ally. There are also one or two minor amendments in several of the other rules which are necessary, and which could be made at the same time. We submit these suggestions for your careful considera- tion, and with every confidence, knowing full well that it is your earnest desire to foster and stimulate a keener interest in everything which appertains to the better management and greater success of the Institute.

The report and balance sheet was adopted. The Chairman said that owing to the Secretary having gone into detail so minutely in the report it left him very little to say, and he could only thank the committee and secretary for the report. Referring to!the decrease in the gate receipts and for bowling, he said we had very little fine weather this summer, there having been only six days during what should have been Coronation week, Mr Ormand had made a very nice statement about the band. They had had to say many times that they were not quite pleased with the band, but now they were pleased with them. They had played many times in the park this summer, and he Loped they would play more when the weather improved. With regard to the rules the committee were of opinion that if the date ot the meeting was altered to o,3tober or November it would be better attended. He, personally, had no objection to the alteration of the date, but it was for them to say whether any alteration should be made. It was then put to the meeting whether the date should be altered, and it was unanimously decided to arrange for the necessary alteration. The Chairman then announced the election of the following, to the Committee of Management, H Bailey, D Harcroft, U Uobeson, II B Schofield, W Smalley and T Ormand. Messrs W Sephton and T S Liversage were re- appointed auditors, and Messrs J Gee and Vir Clare scrutineers.

CHARGE OF CRUELTY AT EARLESTOWN.

A t Newton Petty Sessions on Thursday, Thomas Go re of Haydock, was charged with cruelty to a mare. Inspector Print, of the Royal society, seid be met the defendant with an old brown mare in Market-street, Earlestown, on the 25th ult. It was ' very lame and suffering from contracted tendons. It ought to be killed. The defendant said it was not lame when he started from Havdock ; but had been in the field since he was reported .ind not used at all. The case was adjourned for the opinion of the veterinary surgeon as to whether the animal should be destroyed or not.

THE L. & N.-W. RAILWAY COMPANY AND THE TRAMWAYS.

The "Extraordinary Delay" at Wilderspool.

FULL STATEMENT BY ALDERMAN ROBERTS.

At the Town Council meeting on Tuesday evening Alderman Roberts Wnairman of the Tramways Com- mittee), made a lengthy statement respecting the delay at Wilderspool. Toe minutes of the Committee recorded that a letter was read from Messrs. Preece and Cardew with an estimate of the cost of constructing Railwaa No. 1 under the Light Railway Oteler, and the Town Clerk read a report; on the order. It was resolved that a copy of the report be sent to each member of the committee for consideration at tbe next meeting.— the Light Rail- way Commissioners having issued an order for the con- struction of a light Railway from Victoria.square Stockton Heath, tu Northwich,and having asked tor the observations of the Corporation thereon, it was resolved that no objection be made to the order, as Me clauses affecting the Corporation are in accordance with the terms-agreed upon. Messrs. Preece and Cardew having issued their report on toe tenders received for tram care, it was resolved that the tender of the British Thomson Houston Company. Limited, to supply eleven tram cars at £5,99e, be accepted Messrs Preece and Cardew having informed the com- mittee that the contractors for the new cars were unable to obtain Brill trucks owing to a strike and advising the Substitution of the Brush truck, it was resolved that their recommendation be adopted. Mlssrs Preece and Carden, having advised the lowering of the road so as to obtain a safe clearaace under the Cneshire Lines Bridge over Winivick-tead, it was re s)lved that the recommen- dation be carried out, this committee paying the cost of lowering the road and restoring it to a condition equal to that at present, and that the vies along the south side of Churchastrect be set back so as to enable the carriage-way to be widened at some future date.— Correspondence was read respecting the delay in the construction of the works over the level crossings at Wilderspool. Alderman ROBERTS moved the confirmation of the minutes, gni alluding to the tender of the British Thomson Houston Company, Lim tell, to supply eleven cars, he said they would complre the equipment for the Manchestr-road and Win wick-toad sections. The cars were identical in all respects with those they already had eacept the trucks upon which the cars were to be bu It. The contractors for the new errs were unable to obtain Brill trucks owing to a strike in America, and the sub- stitution of the Brush truck was advis:d by the engin- eers, and it had been decided upon. Tney worked well and gave satisfaction in other towns, and the committee did net think thee would be justified in trusting to the exigencies of the position in allowing an unc rtainty as ' t) the supply of the trucks. They had therefore adopted the Brush Truck. With regard to the reeolution as t) the lowering of the road so as to obtain a safe clearance under the Cheshire Lines Bridge over Win wick-road, it had been thought better to withdraw it SO that they Mght know the cost of the alteration. Toe only reason for having the matter under consideration was the very remote possibility of danger to passengers who might under Some circumstances be consiaerably over fitc in height and who might stand up when they ought not to do when the cars were passing. Al'uding to the delay in the completion of the work at Wilderspool Crossing by the London and North-Western Railway Company, Alderman Reberts said the delay was extraordinary. The committee had had to submit to a good deal ot criticism, friendly and otherwise,for some time past,and as ha l happ snel on more than one occasion, that criti- cism Lied been based up la imperfect knowledge of the I facts, and he therefore proposed to put the Council in I possession of a little information as to what the committee bad tried to do through its officials in connection with this particular question. (Hear, hear. ) he had a letter from Messrs Preece and Cardew, dated the 15th July, whieh should have been read at the ordinary meeting of the C much if it had been held last ween, in which they set out the corres- pondence which had taken place between themselves, ' as the agents of the Corporation, and the London and North-Western Railway Company, exclusively in rerard to this particular opestion. It began on November 9.b, 1900, when Preece and Cardew suggested that the London and North-Western Railway Company should prepare a plan of the crossing. The whole of the ques- tion had been settled by the House of Commons when the Corporation Bill was passed by them, and the res- ponsibility of the work was put upon the London and North-Western Railway Company, who declined to allow anyone but themselves to interfere with their main line. The company were alive to the fact that the work had to be done, and they might have made some pro- vision for carryine it out in something like a reasonable time. Alderman ROBERTS proceeded to give the follow ing additional facts :—Decembsr 13th, London and North Western Rail ay decline to prepare plan, and suggest we should do so. January Bth, 1901, submitted plan to London and Nortu-Westera Railway and at same time forwarded copy to Board of Trade. January 17th, London and North-Western Railway object to our plan and ask for details and model. January 19%, wrote London and Nortla-Western Reilway and pointed out we were willing to arrange organs t i meet any reasonable ' requirements. March 15th, wrote to the London and North-Western Railway asking for reply to our letter of January 19th, and suggested submitting the matter to arbitration. May let, London and North-Western Railway submit plan of crossing :prepared by them. October 4th, 1901, advise London and Nerth•Western Reclwan that Corporation intended to construct tee Wilderspool line, and asked them how thee proposed the work should be carried out, so far as it affected them. January 2nd, 1902. Major ' Cardew met Mr Fletcher in Warrington. January 15tb, again met London and North Western Railway officials in Warrington. January 31st, London and North- Western Railway quote £345 as cost of signals, Ss a February 18th, memorandum respecting signals and safety arrangements agreed, and London and North- , Western Railway asked te proceed with the works. Feb- ruary 20c0, London and N ,rth-Westera Rail way quote £550 for alterations to permanent way and construction of crossing. March 174 h, wrote London and N ,rth. Western Rsilway,accepting their offer of February 20th, to construct crossing. March 21st, London and North- Western Railway an geat an agreement for mainten- ance of works. March 22nd, sent London and North- Western Railway cheque for £550. April 4th, sent London and North-Western Railway cheque for £345. From that date'up to the present there had been a considerable amount of correspondence and telegraph- ing, urging the completion of the work, and only that morning he (Alderman Roberts) sent the followiue wire:—" Dawson, Engineer's Office, London and North-Western Railway Crewe. Most anxious to give 'roan Council at to-night's meeting definite information as to when Wilderspool crossing willibe finished for tramway purposes. Please wire fully at once as to what date we may expect it to be com- pleted. Chairman of the Tramways Committee, War- ringtoo." The reply was as follows :—"Chairman, Tramways Committee Warrington. [Manufacture of crossing will be completed earlier than anticipated in my letter of the 21st, and work on site will probably be commenced in a week or two.—Dawsoet." (" Shame.") He (Alderman Roberts) did not wish to weary the Council with the information but it was desirable they should be put in possession of it that they might see that the committee were business men and bad endeavoured as far as they could to expedite these matters. Be declined to take any responsibility whatever as chairman of the committee for the extraordinary delay on the part of the com- pany in proceeding with the work. He did not wish to offer any further criticism, but leave tbe facts he had adduced before the Council and the public to enable them to form their opinion. With regard to the Light Railway Order from Stafford-road to Victoria-square, all he bad to say was that they had not yet received any intimation that the Parish Council of Stockton Heath desired to have a conference with the Corporation on the question. No doubt they wished to have a confer- ence if the Corporation might judge from the expression of opinion given utt,rance to at their meetings and in their letters to the press, but there was a proper and business-like way of arranging an interview. If these good people of Stockton Heath wished for an interview with the Corporation, it was the simplest thing to write to the Town Clerk through their Clerk asking for it, and it would be at once arranged. What the result would be it would be impossible to foreshadow, but again he declined to be held responsinle for there having been no inter-communication between the parties up to date, and he moved the confirmation of the minutes of the Committee. Councillor BENNETT advocated a double line of tram- ways in Church-street. Be did not think they got the consideration they were entitled to in the east end of Warrington. Perhaps it was because they were in the east end. (Laughter). He bad been hoping that some announcement would be made with respect to the pro- posed extension to Orford-lane. He trusted the com- mittee would be able to accede to what was the almost unanimous request of the residents. The MAYOR : That subject is not on the minutes. Councillor BENNETT proceeded to refer to the delay at the crossing and said he would like to know whether it was absolutely necessary for them to pay the railway Company in advance, and also if there was any power by which they could compel the railway company to go on with their contract. It was contrary to all reason that the Council should fulfil their part of the contract and then be kept waiting by that arbitraty corporation —the railway company. Be felt very ineignant indeed and he thought it was intolerable that even a railway company should have the rower to override the wishes of the inhabitants of a town like Warringtoa so long. (Hear, bear.) Councillor TINNIO N asked if, when the agreement was

made, a clause was not put in as to the time the work should be completed by, and if so had they any remedy to compel the company to have the work 'done immedi- ately ? Tne DEPUTY MAYOR in reply, said the question of the single line tramway in Church-street waB one which could not be discussed then, because their Parliament- ary powers only included a single track. TUC proper time to discuss that and pat forward the claims of those very good constituents of Council/or Bennett was at the time when the whole scheme was under consideration by the Council. They could not alter their scheme now. They had power to take up the Padgate portion,and tee committee had the matter under consideration, but no decision had been come to. Be explained at the last Monthly meeting of tte Council that the London and North-Western Railway Company would not do any work for any corporation on their line for which they were not paid In advance, and those wise-acres outside that Council who knew so much better and had had so much more experience—(aughter)--had something yet. to learn. He spoke tram personal knowledge of the tact They could not get a siding without paying in advance, and as to getting any time limit the company would not listen to sucn a proposal, and there was no power to make them. It was an rx-rernely un- pleasant and aoomalour position to be in. They had to abide by it as well as they could. It waS irritating to a degree almost beyond description. (Hear, heat.) But they had no help for it,and when they dealt with an all-powerful corporat!on like the London and North-Western Railway Company, of course they had to put up with the conditions and make the best of them. Be stlll thought the railway company might have expedited the work, but at the same time he did not think they bad delilleratety delayed it. His own opinion was that it had simply teen treated in its turn. They must make some allowance for the fact that Warring- ton was not the only place which the company had to deal with, and the claims of Warrington were not the only cneo they had to consiell r.• Instead of receiving pe:haps a little extra couSideracicn, which they were entitled to, they had been simply put in the category to take their place and turn as from the day tie company received official notice t g oe with the work. He hoped their lelitesentatione and expressions of opinion, and the general feeling in the town, which was being expressed to tee company bv individu custonsrs, would bring about tee completion of the work at a very early date. (Hear, hear.) Alderman MONKS remark,el tnat the single line tram- way along Ceurch-street had been put in such a position i at if a double line was wanted it could be laid town without any alteration being made. (Hear, hear.) Tee minutes were thee agreed to.

NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTO NN.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT.]

We have had precious little summer, and in the course of a couple of weeks the football season will be upon us. It will be rememb-red that the Ear- lestown Club ha,l a very suczessful run last season, and because of their consistent play.they were well supported, which enabled them to finish up with a pretty fair balance in hand. What they will do in the coming season I am not in a p osition to eay,but from all accounts they are ge•Aiug together a very good ream. They have lost one or two excellent players but have secured othere,whom they say are ' as good, and on the whole they expect to give their supporters a capital exhibition of the gam,.. They have not yet had any practice matches, but it is expected they will get into harness during the next two weeks. I hope next week to give some details showing the composition of the team as it will take the tield in the coming season.

The District Council have evidently seen through the intentions of Lord Newten and his agent in re- gard to hie magnificent offer of a piece of common land, including a pit. as from what can be gathered the stipulated time for the acoeptance of the con- ditional offer, has elapsed and no hing has been done. lam glad the Council have exeecisel some discernment. What were they ts get, and what were they to lose in the getting ?--Evidently the bait dangled before their eyes was not sufficiently inviting for them to take it. The seeming msg. nanimity of the lord of the manor, did not appear Da dissection to be of so generous a character as i did on paper. Consequently the Council have not received the offer so graciously as was perhaps expected. Toe Council have some backbone still, evidently, and we have pr.; got our footpaths.

But what have we IoA ?—We have lost the bury lug ground for dead cats and dogs, an oblong piece of water, which long ago ought to have been filled up, as its presence is a standing danger to children and a nuisance to the people residing near it. Now if Lord Newton wanted to be generous and repay theidistrict somewhat, for what he has received from it, why did he not clean out the Common pit, flag or tile the bottom, cover it in, and then present it to the Council as a swimming bath. Toes is what we want, and if Lord Newton wants to earn our good will, he should see what he can do to improve the district, over which he holds sway as Manorial Lord and for once bury the bogey of con- scription. Noboly in the Newton division wants conscription, but they do want a swimming bath and the Common pit would make an excellent one.

THELWALL WATER.

Dr Adams, mediCa-i-o-tic-3;:iri"Wp-lito a question from Admiral Jones-Parry, said that the brook A t Thelwall, according to the bacteriological report, did not contain actually any diphtheria baci li,bti had the bacilli of the diptheroid group. This bac Li could not be regarded as the actull cause of disease, but it made one regard the water with a certain amount of suspicon, which made him give instructions that the water should be boiled and filtered before using.

Prize Pig's Sad Fate. Arthur Taylor, of Warrington, was summoned at the Liverpool Police Court on Wednesday, for hav- ing deposited tor sale the carcase of a pig which was unfit for human food, it being affected with tuberculosis.—lnspector Wyeberley stated that he found the carcase at the Liver- pool Abattoirs, it having been consigned by the defendant to Messrs. Derkin.—Mr Rudd appeared for the detendant, and said that the animal was a prize one, bir: it met with an accident, injuring its back, and his client bought it, slaughtered it, and sent it to Liverpool. The Court properly considered that Liverpool ought not to be the dumping ground for anything which was un wholesome, but this animal was sent to Liverpoo because it was a boar, and there was a market here for that kind of food. It was a strong kind of food, but thoroughly wholesome for those who liked it.. This pig was not sent because it was unfit for food, but it was sent to Messrs Derkin, a thoroughly re- spectable firm, the defendant not having the least notion that there was anything wrong with it.- -Mr Stewart remarked that it was not a very nice thing that animals should get injured and then be sent to Liverpool as food.—Mr Rudd ; It was killed immediately after.—Mr Stewart : If a person suffers for a few days I presume his condition of body is different to what it was before he met with the accident. There is not EL suspicion of tuber- culosis attached to it if your story is true, but there is the fact that this thing met with an accident.— Mr Rudd: It did, and it was slaughtered im- mediately, just as a cow which breakes its leg is slaughtered immediately so as to preserve what there is. My client had no notion there was any tuberculosis about the pig.—Mr Stewart: A very singular fact.—The defendant said be had no know- ledge that the animal was suffering from tuber- culosis. He bought the pig and paid at the rate of 3i per lb for it. He would not have given more for it if it bad not met with the accident.—A fine of £lO and costs was imposed. BEFORE the MAGISTRATES

A MILE DEALER StlatalONED.-CASE DISafIFSED. An interesting case to farmers and mile dealers was heard at the Warrington Borough Pollee Court on Friday last wesk, before the Mayor (Mr JC Pt rrl and other magistrates. The defendant was Edwar3 Roberts, 43. School-brow, who was summoned for selling milk from whieh it was alleged 2) per cent of cream had been extracted. on July 15th. Mr Browne was for the defence.'-Mr Stanley Wilson, depute town clerk, conducted the case for the prosecutlon and said that on the 15th ult Inspector Flood visited defendant's milkshop and obtained a sample of new milk. He submitted it to the analyst in the usual way and obtained his certi agate on the 23nd of the same month. The certificate went to prove that the sample con Mined nine per nent solids (not fat), 2.4 Solids (fat), 686 tier cent of water. which clearly showed that the milk would be ed a very poor quality. This case was of con- siderable imuortanee to the public, especially because it lent itself to a form of fraud of the most ohjectionable nature. The gondol (fat) were only about half of what they should be in this Instance. He asked that a substan- tial penalty be Inflicted as a warning to othere.- Inspector Flood and Dr Gornall having given evidence, Mr Browne contended that before the proceedings were taken samples of milk should have been taken at the farm where the milk came from, then on its transit, and then from the dealer. The quality of milk in the morning was different from that in the fevening.- The Mayor (Mr J C Parr) said he thought it was a pity there Was a discrepancy of that kind.-Mr Browne said the farmers complained that it was impossible to keen up the standard all the year at 3 percent. It went as low on same occasions as 1.8. and he would prove it. On behalf of the farmers and milk dealers, who only wanted fairness, he urged that all the samples he token before any prosecution was commenced. This was one of the most important clues- iions of the day. In fact be had heard some farmers say that if those prosecutions went on-it being absolutely impossible to keep up the standard of three per cent, all the year roand -they would give up keeping cows and not send milk into the town. The suggestion of the Board of Agriculture in thi3 matter went to show that they were fully aware of the difficulty of keeping up the standard. He would call a number of witnesses first. Mr Roberts, the defendant, whose father and great grandfather had been for 80 years in the Same business, with not a single complaint against them. If the cream in this ease had been abstracted, it must have been abstracted by someone. He would nrove that Mr Roberts never Saw the milk. The only persons in the house were his wife and 'sister. who would prove that they never extracted any cream. Mr Rritch, the farmer who supplied the milk,would tell them that he milked. 55W it put in the tankard, and fastened up. He sent it to Warrington by a boy who could not have abstracted it on the way. Therefore, where could the abstraction come in ? Neither Mr Rotarrts nor Mr 33ritch had a separator. The former did not sell cream. nor did he churn. The only thing was" were the cows guilty?' as the Magistrates' Clerk asked on the last occasion. (Laughter). Were the Stench going to hold respectable re- tailers and farmers gnEtv of haring abstractei something they had never done? It Was the height of absurdity. The question of taking samples was a matter which should be thoroughly thrashed out, so that they would knc,av where they were in the future. EvidEnce having been called in support of Mr l3rowne's statement, the Bench retimcl to consider the case. and en ' their return the Chairmen said they were unanimous in dismissing the case without costs, RS they were not satisfied that cream had been abstracted. They thought that other samnies might have been taken in accordance with the slog. gestion of the memorandum of the Board of Agriculture. They thought the system used in Manchester a very good one and might he adopted in Warrington.--Mr Wilson said be would bring the recommendation before .the Health Com- mittee. He gave notice of appeal acCording to his instructions. CAUGHT IN THE LANDLORD'S SITTIrsm-Room. At the Warrington Borough Police Court on Wednesday, before Mr. T. NORTHERN UNION CUP.

First Round.

For nest season the Northern Union have decided upon making the draw for the Cup-ties on similar lines to those adopted by the Football Association, and it is generally thought that the new departure will prove more satisfactory all round. On Tuesday evening, at the Bachange Hotel, Manchester, the draw for the first round was made, and some ef the ties will provoke a desi of interest especially that wherein Broughton Rangers are drawn against Oldham at Wheater's Field. MARRIAGE AT CHRIST CHURCH

LATCH FORD.

Lowe—Griffiths.

A large congregation assembled in Christ Church, Latchford, on Tuesday morning to witness the wedding of Mr Ed win Ernest Lowe, F.L.S., curator of the Plymouth Museum and Art Gallery (formerly assistant curator at the Warrington Museum, and only son of Mr and Mrs E W R Lowe, of Warrington and Great Sankey), and Miss Maude Griffiths, eldest daughter of Mr and Mrs J R Griffiths, of the Cheshire Cheese Inn, Latchford. The bride, who was given away by her father, wore a magnificent gown which was a present from a brother in Chicago. where it was cut to shape and embroidered by the Wilson Embroidery Company of that city. The material is a beautiful quality of duchesse satin, embroidered with real Chantilly lace, embedded with clusters of pearls, the design of the gown being by Doucet, of Paris. She carried a very pretty shower bouquet of white exotics. The bridesmaids were Miss R M Lowe (sister of the bridegroom) and Miss Lily Griffiths (sister of the bride), who wore muslin delaine dresses with black picture hats. Miss Maude Dorothy Griffiths (niece), Miss Ethel May dley (niece), and Miss Maude Gladys Llewellyn (niece), acted as train bearers. The bridesmaids carried shower bouquets fashioned of sweet peas. Mr Llewellyn Griffiths (brother of the bride) acted as " best man." The officiating clergyman was the Rev F A Screeton, vicar of the paris'n. Mr Charles R Bradley, organist of St Mary's Parish Church Bootle, and who is a cousin of the bride,was at the organ and contributed amongst other music, before during, and after the service, a very pretty melody, the" March IN uptiale," by Bernard, the Wedding March ' from " Lohengrin," and Mendelssohn's " Wedding March," the latter as the newly-married couple left the church. They were subjected to the usual showers of rice and confetti. At the conclusion of the ceremony the newly- married couple and the wedding guests sat down to an excellent breakfast, at the residence of the bride's father, about 50 being present. The health of the bride and bridegroom,and their parents were drunk with enthusiasm. Mr James Parkinson, of Egypt-street, was in attendance and, in the garden attached to the house, took several photographic groups of the bride and bridegroom, and the rela- tives and friends. Later in the day, the happy couple. amid the best wishes of all present, left for South Devon. where the honeymoon is to ba spent. The following is a list of the Presents . THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY AUGUST 15 1902

===== 0002601_19020815_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
VvARRINGTON TRADES AND LABOUR COUNCIL.

The Corporation Fair Wage Clause.

There was a representative attendance at the meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labaur Council held in Um Druids Hall, Sankey-street, on Tuesday evening. Mr Edwardson,president of the Council, occupied the chair.

===== 0002601_19020815_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. PARKER AND THE HOUSING PROBLEM.

Mr C E PARKER, of Penketh, in calling the attention of the Council to the conference of the Garden City Association said there was no doubt that the housing problem was of Immense imprmtance, affecting as it did, the hearth, the vitality,the morals and the general well- being of the community. Many of the houses in War- rington were a diggrace to our civilization but it was no use pulling these down until we could replace them with other houses at a reasonable rent. The cost of land and the rates in towns made low rents impossible. Whether by securing cheap land and building on a large and systematic scale we could reduce rents to any great extent he did not know. But even if rents for the same capacity of house were the same the improv:d surroundings would hN a great gain. And if the Garden City Association could show us the way it might poseible to do something in Wirrington. Electric trac- tion bad made this thing more practicable. "43The SECRETARY reported that he had replied to the letter of Mr Parker and informed'him that the Council although sympathising with the object of the Association were unable to send a representative to the conference. THE PROPOSED VISITS TO THE WORKHOUSE.

At a meeting of the Executive Committee of the Council, tha secretary was instructed to write to the Earlestown Trades Council asking that body to co- operate with the Warrington Trades and Labour Council in endeavouring to prevail on the Guardians to acceie to thir request for permission to pay visits to the Workhouse. _ . _ .

In renly, the SECRETARY of the Earlestown Trade Council (Mr William Speakman) said he would bring the matter before the next meeting of the Council. He thought they would be able to co-operate with the War- rington Council. PETITION AGAINST THE EDUCATION BILL.

A communication was read before the Executive Com- mittee from the Bradford Trades Coumcil askinz the Warrington Council to petition against the Government Education Bill, It was decided to forward petition against the bill to the HORSP of Common.. The petition ran as iollows : —" Your petitioners being deeply concerned in ths education of the children of the working classes desire to call for the withdrawal of the E lucation Bill now be- fore your honourable house. Your petitioners believe the changes admitted in the Education Bill have transformed it to what is practically a new measure and the country should therefore have time to consider it. Your peti- tioners believe that in the interests of educeion, the Bill before your honourable house should be withdrawn, and a committee of members of Parliament be selected from all sections of the house,including labour members, to draft a National Education Bill. Your petitioners therefore pray that your honourable house will be pleated to take such steps as may be deemed expedient to this end. Signed on behalf of the I,Varrineten Trades and Labour Council, J Edwardson, president, and F Stott, secretary." BEWSEY IRONWORKERS AND THE COUNCIL.

A letter was read from Mr C Algie, secretary of . the Bewsey Ironworkers Association stating that the mem- bers desired to withdraw from the Council on the ground that their association would not allow the members of the Trades and Labour Council to step in between them and the firm if anything occurred to warrant it.

• I The SECRETARY pointed nut that the ironsvorke,s did not seem to understand what wero the objects of the Trades and Labour Council. I Mr GREALEY said he could not remember the time when the Trades and Labour Council interfered in a dis- pute. They never did go uoL,ss they were asked. Mr. HAMBLETT thouaht there wasl'an undercurrent against the Counci'. Nothing had occurred to warrant the reason given for withdrawing from the Council. CLAUSE.

Writing on the 18'h July, Mr J Lyon Whittle (town clerk) said he did not know what was the intention of the Corporation with regard to the three contracts r.- ferred to by Mr Stott. He could not express .any opinion with regard to the contracts as he had received no instructions from the Connell. He might, however, say with regard to one of the firms, he had personally made inquiries and was aesured that they p 37 the rate of wages and observe the hours of labour recognised in the district. It occurred to him that if the Trades Coun- cil had any evidence concerning the contracts referred to, they should send a statement of facts to be laid before the cammittres concerned. In reply, Mr STOTT said that what the Trades and Labour Conncil would like to know was whether tha Town Council were oF.the opinion that the three firms named in their letter paid the rate of wages, and observe the hours of labour recognised in the trade in the.district, As stated in a former letter, they knew of no recognised rate except that which had been mivei- ally agreed to leo the trades unions employere. Was that the standard reco.grolsed by the Trades Council ? If it Was not, would the Town Clerk kindly Far in which war they ascertained what was •• the recognised standard," or whether it was left to the contractor to abate that he paid the rate of wages and observed the hours of labour recognised in the district for that par- ticular trade. The TOWN CLERK in a further letter pointed out that the three contracts referred to were entered into before it was decided by the Council to insert the fair wage clause in all contracts. Nothing could now be gained by enquiring as to the rate of wages and hours of labour under the three contracts referred to.lt could not be ex- pected that the Town Council could set up a court of en viry into all contracts entered into by tbem. The fair wage clause would be inserted'in all contracts in which it could be operative and if it could be proved that a con- tractor was not paying the rate of wage and observing the hours of labour recognised in the district, the con- tract might be cancelled. If from the facilities at the Trades Council's command they could furnish evidence en.this point with regard to any future contract the Corporation would no doubt cancel the contract.

The SECRETARY observed that the two !letters from the Town Clerk were very good. Ha believed Mr Lyon Whittle had stated very fairly, the opinion of the Town Council. It was rather absurd for the Corporation to say that the three contracts were entered into before the Town Council decided to insert the fair wage clause in all contracts becauss:he(Mr Stott)had letters from the Town Clerk stating that the resolution passed by the Council eleven years ago was being strictly adhered to. That was rather inconsistent with the statemPnt now made. It was very interesting and instructive to read these letters.

Mr BUCKLEY congratulated the socretary upon the way he had conducted the corre.pondenc., and said he thoroughly deserved the thanks of the delegates. They had gained some good for they had found out that the Town Council had not hitherto inserted the fair-wage clause in contract.. It was satisfactory to learn. that they intended to do so in future contracts. The SECRETARY said it would be advisable for the Trades Council to produce evidence when they com- plained of any Corporation contract being given to a firm which did not cooaply with the conditions laid down.

Mr PLINSTOM said they must hold a watching brief for future action. The subject then dropped. THE DISPUTE AT GIBRALTAR.

LOYAL ORDER OF ANCIENT SHEPHEaDS A.U.

The members of the Warrington Juvenile Burial Society and the Widow and Orphans Fund assembled at the British Workman Public House, Eforsemarket-street, on Monday, the occasion beine the 31st annual meet- ing in cosnection with the Juvenile Burial Society and the 37th of the Widow and Orphans Fund. The attend- ance of the members of both funds compared very favourably wita the crowded meetings of past years and manifested the keenest interest in the whole of the evening's proceedings. The meeting was presided over by the president of the Societies, PP CS John Haley and was supported by the vice-presid- ent, DPCS Joseph Wi'liams, P PCS James Parr, treasurer, PPCS Lawrence Weir, general secretary and Trustees, P M William Holmes, PPC S William Eckersley, PPC S Peter Eiger, PP CS Ttiorriss PPCS Frederick Massey, P PCS William Hall, P PCS James Lowe, P PCS Henry Warburton, PPC S James Greity, and D PCS Joseph Adams.—The Chairman expressed the gratifica- tion it afforded him to have the honour of presiding over this their annual meeting of the two Sacieties. He said the time has again corns round when in presenting oir annual report and balance sheet, we take the liberty of not only making a statement of our past I doings, but also of taking the bearings of our present ! position and future prospects. Many things have con- tributed to our past success, and not least of these must be inenlioned to the able and zealous labours of our es- teemed and worthy General Secretary,backed up by the, earnest and vigilant care of the Committee of I Management. An estimate of this may be found in the fact that during the past nine years working up to the present time, the Society has more than doubled the ceptifel, which had previously been accumu- lated from its formation in 1871 to 1893 (a period of ' 22 years). This alone speaks for itself as a testimony of the flourishing condition of the Saciety,and of the sound- ness of the principles of management. Let us take again another point of view in cinsidering the success of the Society's workings. With a present membership of almost 1,300 the average cost per year for the man- agement expenses is not more than 5 per cent in excess of the average cost for the preceding 22 years, which speaks volumes for the economy exercised in its present management. Small wonder that we are proud of our juvenile societies based on similar positions to our own, but we most not forget that such a high standard of efficiency possesses also its dangers. First the indulgent apathy it may beget in its management. Let us not therefore rest con- tented with our labours and rest on our oars, but let us still be up and doing for greater achievements and wider conquests, for the cause is a noble one. But while our Society is thus doing a good work for our javenile population we have not been unmindful of kindred societies whese operations and benefits;have naturally extended beyond our own as is shown in the fact 'hat we have paid the " entrance fees "of upwards ' of 160 of our members t) the adult lodges of the Society whose name we ar, pr Rid to bear, thus durine, the nine years I have named we have spent some £l6 or £l7 in this way, and assisting them in other ways until they ar3 full members in the adult order.—The General Secretar? P.P.C.S. Lawrence Weir in submitting the annual balance sheet showed that the total worth of the Juvenile Burial Society now amounted to £2,470 13s 91, as compared with £2,275 59 51 for the corresponding psriod last year, thus making a gain upon the year's traneactions of £195 83 4d,with a total mein- bersnin of 1,291. Toe report further showed that the Widow and:Orphaos' Fund had a capital of £206 12.311 e, as compared with £205 181 91 in the previous year, making a gain in this fund of 144 23. The Secretary said the president in his remarks referred to the progress this Burial Society had made during the past nine years. lie was proud to inform them it bad made a gain during that period of no less a sum than £1,391 4s 4cl, or an average of £154 us 73 or thereabouts per year. He further stated the total income for interest alone came to L'34 63 6d per year, and would exceed that amount at the end of the year. They were looking forward to theo time when the working expenses will be bourne entirely by the interest of the invested capital. He said these results of their labour had not been brought about without perseverance from all concerned upon the com- mittee of management and urged them not to be centent until they had placed thernselvee in such a financial position that would meet any emergency that might take place. The auditors PPC S Peter Edgar and P 51, John Jones in giving their report of their labours in connec- tion with the accounts, stated that the various accounts and registers kept by the general secretary and also the treasurer and colleaors' books had been examined, and found correct. In the election of officers Bre Joseph Williams P.C.S. Was elected: president and Bra Joseph Adams D.P.C.s. vice-president, P.P.C.S. Lawrence Weir general secretary, P.P.C.S. James Parr treasurer, Bros William Holrnee,Peter Edgar,and William Eckersley Trustees, P.M. John Jones, was re-elec- ted auditor,Committee of Management Bros John Jones, Frederick Massey, William Slevin, Henry Cook, Thomas Miller, Samuel Bradshaw, William Hall. John Haley, David Harris, Henry Warburton, James 'Gresty. The reinainder'of the agenda consisted of several matters of importance bearing upon the general welfare of tho societies, the principal being to take into consideration the question of forming a juvenile demonstration at some future date, and to vote such a sum as will cover the necessary expenses of carrying out the same. This was unanimously passed. The principal speakers were: Brothers Thomas Miller, John Jones, Peter Edgar, Jame Parr, William Hall,Joseph Adams, Samuel Blythe, James Lowe, and others. A vote of thanks was accorded the retiring president P.P.C.S. John Haley, for the admir- able manner in which he had conducted the business during his term of office. Brother John Haley in response thanked the members for their kind appreciation of his services and assured them his work bad been a labour of love—and he should endeavour to still do what he could for the interest of the societies. The meeting afterwards terminated.

FOUND DEAD IN APPLETON

DINGLE.

The Inquest. Recommendation by the Jury.

Early on Sunday morning a man named William Jepson, of Rose Bank, Stockton ileatn was on the bridge which crosses Dingle trook at Appleton when he noticed a man lying dead in the shallow water beneath the bridge. The deceased's forehead was terribly bruised and cut and the man had evidently been dead some hours. The police were communicated with, and it was ascertained that the body was that of John Hindley, 28 years of age, a wheelwright, who worked in his lather's shop at Appleton. The deceased, who was unmarried, attended the Coronation festivities in Walton Park on Saturday evening and after witnessing the dis- play of fireworks, left for home accompanied by a friend who went with him part of the journey.

The inquest was held at the Thorn Inn, App-leton Thorn, by Mr R Dobson, coroner, on Tuesday even- ing. Jonas Hindley, father of the deceased, said his son lived with him at Wright's Green, Appleton. He was 28 years of age, and enjoyed very good health. He saw him about eight o'clock on Saturday night, when he went away in a governess zar with a man named William Tipping. lie did not see him again alive. William Starkie Brodie, Appleton, gamekeeper, said he met Hindley at the Cat and Lion Hotel, at about 'ialf-past eight. No one was with him. Hindley had three glasses of beer and they then went towards Walton. That was about half-past nine. They went to Walton Park to see the fire- works and left shortly before eleven. They went along by Hughe:'s Bridge and witness left him at Lester's gate. The deceased went towards home. He was quite sober. William Jepson, of Rose Bank, Stockton Heath, said that on Sunday morning he went for a walk ly the quarry with a friend. When he got to the bridge he looked over, and saw the body of a man in the water. It was face downwards, and the mouth was in the water. They gave the alarm to Mr Claydon. the woodman. Information was sub- sequently Riven to the police. There was a loose I stone near the coping. John Chtvdon, who resides at Dingle Lodge, de- posed to going to the bridge and seeing the body. Sergeant Bourne said he recovered the body. The bead was on the edge of a large stone, and there was a wound on the forehead just over the left eye. It was about 11 inches deep. There was a wound on the right head and an abrasion on the knee. The night in question was dark and rough. The wall leading to the bridge was 22 inches high, and that was the only safeguard to passengers using the road. The deceased would fall about 15 feet. The body was removed and searched. 'WI. juror called attention to the wall, and said that in the bridge had been fenced the accident might not have happened. It was very dangerous, especially on a dark night. 14 The jury returned a verdict to the effect that deceased died from injuries to the head, apparently received through a fall from the bridge, and recom- mended the Coroner to write to the proper authority with reference to the approach to the bridge.

WARRINGTON.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS. Appointment of Registrar.

A meeting of this Board was held in the Board Room at the Workhouse on Wednesday. Dr Patterson pre- sided and there were also present :—Mrs Clay ,Revs T II Matthews, J H Riddette, Dr Murray, Messrs A Brooks, A J Jolley, A Jolley, G Pilling, J Billinge, E L Bent, J W Knowles, P Longron, T H Ashcroft, R Forshaw, J Clarke, J Payton, T Halton, J Robinson, J Ball, William Houghton, T Ritchie, J Backhouse, G Mather, with J C Sutton (clerk), 8 Sutton, (assistant clerk), F Gate (master), and A Wright (superintendent of schools). T E MAYOR AND THE PADGATE SCFIJOL

===== 0002601_19020815_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
CHILDREN,

Mr J CHARLTON PARR (Mayor) wrote 28 follows : " August 12, 1902. Visited Padgate Schools. The Mayoress and I were much impressed with the cheery look of everything and everybody and also with the neat and orderly appearacce of the children and their look of happiness and health,"

HOUSE COMMITTEE. ELECTRIC LIGHT EXTENSIONS.

The minutes of the House Committee included re- commendations that the tender of Messrs Morton and Sampson for extension of the electric light for the sum of £59 10s be acceptrd ; that a sketch plan and speci- fication for formation of a new airing yard for male lunatics be approved and tenders applied for and that sundry repairs be effected. The minutes were approved on the motion of Mr A. BROOKS.

AN ABSENTEE MEMBER.-AN ELECTION DELLARED.

The CLERK submitted a declaration signed by threel members ot the Guardians (Messrs G Pilling, J Back- house and G Mather; to the effect that Mr Peter Cunliffe, of 366. Church-road, Haydock, elected in 1201 as a Guardian of the poor for the East Ward of the Parish of Haydock having been absent from meetings of the Board for more than six months consecutively, and the oppor- tunity of explanation of the cause of his absence having been afforded without any explanation being received from him, the Board declared the office vacant, and it was ordered that the Clerk forthwith take the necessary steps to fill the vacancy. _ _ _ _

The Rev J H RIDDETTE said be WIS sorry Mr Cunliffe had not sent an explanation. Was it necessary to pro- ceed to an election ? It was only six months until the proper election came round again. The CLERK pointed out that the election was 18 months off.

Mr RIDDETTE said he was under a misapprehension about it. He quite thought they WE nt out of office next March.

The CLERK stated that if there was no opposition there would be no cost to the parish, but if there was a con- tested election all the costs would fall on the parish of Haydock. . . . .

The CLERK wag inet,ucted to proceed with the election. SANKEY SUB-REGISTRATION DISTRICT. APPOINTMENT OF REGISTRAR.

A communication was read by the CLERK from the Registrar General with reference to the appointment of a registrar for the sub-district of Sankey, enclosing a copy of a letter from the Buttonwood Parish Council, and requesting that the Board of Guardians would favour him with their opinion as to the necessity or desirability of taking the present opportunity to make any change in the constitution of that sub-district. He stated that he would not be prepared to entertain any proposal that would increase the present number of sub- districts into which Warrington Registration was divided. It appeared that the whole of the Sankey sub-district formed part of the relief district for which Mr Bowes (registrar of births and deaths for Newton-in-Makerfield sub-district) acted as relieving officer. The letter enclosed from the Burtonwood Parish Coun- cil was as follows :—To the Registrar General, July 10th, 1902. Honoured sir,—.On the information that Mr B Hobson, registrar to e Sankey sub-registration district, has tendered his resignation, my Council feel justified in again approaching you with regard to a sub- registration office in this township, and having in view your letter of July 2n,1, 1900, have instructed their repreeentative on the Board of Guardians to bring this matter again before them, and should their decision be favourable my Council pray that you will grant this long felt want to the township.—(Signed) JCWN Fain- moron (clerk).. The CLERX said be had advertised for a registrar in accordance with instructions, and had received 14 applications for the post. The Local Government Eoard hoped that the Guardians would in future see that the duties of the vaccination officer were properly and efficiently carried out. All they could do was to appoint a capable man, who required not only to be a Rood writer but one who could properly conduct a case before'the magistrates if necessary. Mr J. W. KNOWLES : A man with a business training, you might say. The CLERK said be had received the following letter from the Burtonwood Parish Council :—" July 29th. Dear sir,—At a convened parish meeting held in the Board Schools yesterday,and which was largely attended by latepayers, the following resolution was unanimously carr;ed : ' That this meeting is of opinion that great convenience would be given to the ratepayers of Burton- Wood ,if the Warrington Guardians would transfer Buttonwood from the Sankey sub-registration district ; failing that, that they appoint a registrar to reside in al central position to the whole district.' I am also in- structed to point out the following : (1) That doctors residing' in Earlestown attend nine-tenths of sick cases in Burtonwood. (2) That insurance agents living in Earlestnwn have nine-tenths of the business in Burton wood. (3) That Earlestown is the market town for the whole of the working classes. (4) That there is a good train service to Earlestown. (5) That a considerable saving of time and expense would be made if registra- tion could be done in Earlestown.—Yours truly JonN Fazactouoir (clerk). J. C. Sutton, Esq." Mr Fonsnew remarked that Earlestown would suit their district the best, and be wanted to ask the Clerk if it was possible for Buttonwood to be added to the Earlestown registration district. The CLERK said this would alter the district, and the effect would be that it would not be worth while for al man to take up the position of registrar for the other' pnrtion of the district. The office at present was worth £25 for registration purposes. There was probably £l5 in addition to that for fees, so that the registration of the distric:: would probably produce £4O. The vaccina- tion fees bad been £5O, and due attention had not been accorded, in the judgment of the Loral Government Board, to vaceination,so that the emoluments would be much larger for vaccination in all probability. They might take it that the joint sum would reach about £lOO a year, and, as he would be able to do other work, they ought to find a very good man, who would so regu- late his duties as to avoid all difficulties.

Mr KNow LES proposed. and Mr PILLING seconded,that Mr Joseph Smith. of Great SankPy, be appointed to the office.

An amendment was mowed by Mr Bnoorcs and second- ed by Mr FORSHAW, that it be left to a sub-committee to select two or three of the candidates to appear before them at the next meeting. The CHAIRMAN said be supported Mr Bros's propo- sition. He thought they should go into the merits of each man.

After some discussion, Mr KNOW LES said if it would please everyone he was willing that all the names of the candidates should be put to the meeting and voted upon. This was eventually agreed upon. The following were the candidates whrse names were submitted and voted upon .—Messrs John T Gerrard, Crow-lane, New- ton ; John -8 &anther°, Market-street, Earlestown ; J G Pownall, collector of taxes, Great Sankey ; John Fairclough, assistant overseer, Burtonwood ; John Rains, Clipsley-lane, Haydock ; Thomas E Clough, Earlestown ; D Kitshaw,book-treeper,Manehester ; Ralph Log, Sankey ; E Hobson, l'enketh ; E Foster, assiszant overseer, Penketh ; Joseph Smith, Great Sankey ; J W Molyneux, Nantwich J R Jones, Padgate Schools ; and Arthur W Shearer. The result of the voting by a show of hands was that Mr Joseph Smith, of Groat Sank, y, received the highest numlrr of votes, viz 17. Mr Jones was second with 11 ; and Mr Long third with 8. The final voting resulted in Mr Smith defeating Mr Jones by 11 votes to 8.

Mr smith was irpnintod subjort to his appFarine at tho nest meeting of ths Board and aereoing to condi- tions as to his residence being in the centre of the die- trim.

This was all thetusiness and the Board rose.

TIIE EXAMINER FRIDAY AUGUST 15, 1902

jar and all approached the work of the Bqard in a busi- ness like spirit.. (Applause.) . .

Mr Taylor said, the previous speakers had taken the wind out of his sails. (Laughter.) Being the third speaker he was not in a position to say much. But it gave him extreme pleasure to be with them, and partici- pate ia such an 'enjoyable gathering. He felt it was a pity they could not have such sociale a little oftener. If ' they would only arrange one every three months he promised them they would have seven Guardians from Runcorn to attend the meet- ings with the utmost regularity. (Laughter.) They would observe that at present there was some difficulty in getting the necessary seven from Runcorn, but the difficulty could be entirely obviated by acting on his suggestion. (Laughter.) His reason for going to the Board meetings for so many years was because he delighted in looking after the poor. (Applause). Other- wise he would not trouble to go. It was a cast in time and sacrifice for it took almost a full day, but he delighted to go for the reason stated. (Applause), As he looked round the Board he thought bow much more generously they would have dealt with the out-relief if the applications had been made after the dinner. (Laughter.) It would have gladdened the hearts of the relieving officem The meetings did much to bring them in touch with one another and it vies a pleasure to en- deavour to do one's duty for those who needed it. (Ap- plause.) Mr Taylor rroposcd the health of Mr and Mrs Cunnah. In a highly eulogistic speech Mr Taylor spoke of the good qualities of the master and matron and said they could not possibly be better qualified for their respective position.—Mr Cunnah briefly replied. Mr Stokes proposed the health of the Clerk,Mr Ashten, and the deputy clerk, Mr Hough, to which Mr Ashton briefly responded. ===== 0002601_19020815_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
CORONATION FESTIVITIES

(Continued from page 3.) Earleatown.

Beyond a slight display of bunting, no steps were taken in Esrlestown or Newton to celebrate the Corona- tion. In fact had there been any intention to indulge in any kind of festivities, there would have been but a poor show, in consequence of such a large number of people having departed for other places. On Sunday special Coronation services were held in the three parish churches, there being fairly good congregations at each.

===== 0002601_19020815_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
The Celebrations at Runcorn.

On Saturday a demonstration of the friendly societies was held at Runcorn in commemoration of the King's coronation. The day was all that could be desired for a spectacular dieplay and the proceedings were a marked succees. Some of the tradespeople closed a portion of the day, others the whole of the day but the majority did not prefer to make the best business day of the week into a holiday. Thoce who were to take part in the Friendly Societies procession began to assemble in the Market-place shortly after half-past two. The members of the Duke's Club, who took the initiative in arranging the demonstration, ' had met on the lawn of Bridgewater House. Banquet at Dutton Workhouse.

In connection with the King'd coronation a banquet was given by Mr 9 Taylor chairman of the Runcorn Urban District Council, Mr Knowles. chairman of the Rural District Council and Mr J Stokes chairman of the Board of Guardians to the members of the Rural District Coun- cil and Board of Guardians on the accession of the King to the throne. The unforeseen illness of the King pre- vented the projected banquet fre in taking place at the rime appointed and was at length arranged to be held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday last. The Council Chamber, which bad been most elaborately and artistically decorated by Mr and Mrs Cuanah master and matron, had been requisi- tioned and nothing had been omitted to ensure the suc- cess of the proceedings. At the bead of the board sat Mr Taylor, Mr Knowles and Mr Stokes ani with one or two exceptions the Council Chamber was honoured with a full attendance of the representatives of the two authorities with the officials. After a dinner worthy of the COMmemoraticn of so memorable an event the cus- tomary loyal toasts were given by Mr Knowles in felicitous terms and were responded to right heartily. Mr Knowles then said that the next business was a small presentation to Mr Moseley-Williams. They were all pleased to see him. He had been a member of the Rural Council and the Board for a great number of years, and had rendered yeoman service. (Applause). Mr estokea, in making the presentation said that as chairman of the Board of Guardians it afforded him great pleasure to mark the esteem in which Mr Williams was held. Mr Williams removal from Frodshana, which place he represented, would mean a great loss toithe township and to the Beard generally. It was Mr Williams whom he (the speaker) had followed in the position be then held a position which he (Mr Williams) filled with honour and dignity, He was not really leav- ing them although he was leaving the neighbourhood and he hoped he would be with them for many years to come, He congratulated him upon the happy occasion which bad called forth the presentation. (Applause.) He trusted that Mr and Mrs Williams, would be lone spared to the happiness which they would enjoyein their future married life and that each would live • long for each other in comforts and trials. He trusted Mr Williams would accept the small present as a testimony to the existing spirit between them, the kind regards and the pleasant memories which might never be forgotten. The inscription read as follows :—" Pre- sented to J Moseley Williams, &el, by the members and the officers of the Runcorn Union and District Council on the occasion 31 his marriage, 1902." It was with great pleasure be banded the small present—a silver cigarette case, Ste,—to Mr Williams, which would be a link between him and the Runcorn Union. (Applause). Mr Williams said that the kind words of the Chairman of the Council and the Chairman of the Beetle of Guard- , isms had quite taken the steam out of him. He had always attended the meetings as the Frodsham representative with the greatest amount of pleasure. He bad come into contact with those who had ' always give him a pleasant Word and a kindly welcome. He hoped his experience of life coupled with his business experience had bo3n of some Ilse. They would remem- ber that at the time of the small-pox epidemic he with Dr Adams, was responsible for the spending of £l,OOO in sanitary measures. At the time he was censured by the ratepayers as being extravagant, but he was sure that those in that room felt that money spent on sani- tary matters was money well laid out Re thanked them tor the great amount of consideration shown to him during the three years he held office as chairman of the Board. He had no intention to give up the representation of Frodsharn entirely at present. If he were to resign it would cost £2O to 230 in the event of an election. With the consent of the Frodsharia people be would repressnt them until his term expired which would be in about 12 toonthe. He heartily thanked the three gentlemen who had invited him to the banquet and for the honour done to him. (Applause). Mr Woodward proposed the health of Mr Knowles, the chairman of the Rural Council, Mr 9 Taylor, chair- man of the Runcorn Urban Council, and Mr Stokes, chairman of the Board of Guardians. In an able speech Mr NVnorlward eulogised the public work of the gentle- men who were all the right men in the right place. While Mr Knowles' genielite and urbanity made him an excel- lent chairman,Mr Stokes' straight forwardness a capital business man, words failed him to express his apprecia- tion of Mr Taylor's serbice. LIYLL ORDER OF KNCIENT BREPHERDS.-,WARREgGTON

DISTRICT.

_ The Late Spring Rose Lodge, after taking part in the procession, spent a pleasant hour at their club house, Barley Mow Inn. After the usual vote of thanks to the Chairman, the meeting was closed by singing the National Anthem. GRAND UNITED ORRER OF ODDFELLOWS' TREAT.

After the procession the members ot the Grand United Order of Oddfellows proceeded to the Assembly rooms of the Conservative Club, Winmarleigh street, where pies and other refreehments were served. A tea and meet- ing to which the wives of the members were invited, took place at three o'clock. The toast of " the King" was proposed by Ern R GIBSON, D.M., who feelingly alluded to the illness of his Majesty, and hoped he would long be spared to reign. The toast of " Queen Alexandra, the I rince and Princess of Wales, and the rest of the Royal Family " was also proposed by the D.M., who raferred to the anxious time the Queen had passed through. Ho trusted she would have a bright and happy future. Bra H WELSBY, district relieving officer, as an old Volunteer next proposed " The Army, Navy, and the Reserve Forces." Speaking of the Volunteers and Yeomanry particularly be remarked that In his time, and up till lately, they were spoken of as "Saturday night soldiers," but in thefuture he ventured to say that could not be said as the action of the Volunteers and Yeomanry and proved the contrary, and no doubt might have caused other countries to think twice before coming to blows with tbis country. (Applause). The DISTRICT SECRETARY proposed "The Grand United Order of Oddfe'lows and the Warrington Dis- trict," and advised all young members to attend their lodge meetings and take an active part in the manage- ment, and also to try and get young men to join the Order.

The DISTRICT MASTER having to leave, the DEPUTY DISTRICT MASTER (J T Griffiths) respended, and also to a vote of thanks to the Chairman, lie asked all lodge officers to be energetic in their duties, and to encourage the young members by their regular attendance at the lodge me.-tings. He thanked the ladies for their attend- ance' and hoped they had enjoyed themselves. The National Anthem was then sung, and the meeting concluded with cheers.

During the evening rrefreshcnents were provided free of charae to members and their wives. Songs we:e sung by Mr Cotton, who was accompanied on the piano by Miss Cotton, and bc several others who were accom- panied by Mr James Gsrst, the blind pianist, through the kind permission of Mrs Plinston of the King's Arms.

T EIE ILLUMINATIONS.

The illuminations in the town were on a very modi- fied scale. The Coronation funds wer, insufficient to provide for the illumination of the Town Hall and grounds and there was general disappointment that the brilliant display of June last was not repeated. The Museum and the new police building were again illumia- ated and many went to inspect them. A device con- taming the words "God save the King" was also erected on the top of the electricity station and several trades- men illuminated their premises. In Arpley -street, there was an effective Illumination of the Nurses Homes the words "E "and "A" being outlined in coloured fairy lamps. There were also illuminations on a small scale at a number of private houses, but the illumination of he town cannot be said to have beea general. PULPIT REFERENCES. BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN CFLUSCEL

Special Coronation services were held at Bold-street Wesleyan Church on Sunday. In the morning the service began with the singing of " God save the King." The Rev T Bishop (superintendent of the circuit) was the preacher, and in the course of his remarks referred to the recovery of the King in answer to the prayers of his people, and said his Coronation was fait accompli. A special Coronation hymn was sung, and the solo " Rejoice greatly " was ably rendered by Mrs Booth. In the evening the Rev C El Hocken preached his fare- well sermon to the people of Bold-street, and the choir sang with effect the Hallelujah chorus. WYCLIFFE CHURCH. Mr. A. Corney occupied the pulpit, morning and evening at Wycliffe Congregatior al Church on Sunday. In the morning he preached from the text "Thou settest a crown of pure geld on his bead" (Psalm 21, v 3). The preacher drew an interesting comparison between the Psalmist's experiences and those of King Eiward vIL. The band of God, he said, bad been upon this nation during its recent trial,but it had been the band of Love. With chastened joy they rejoiced in the coronation of the King. The beautiful message of the King to his people proved that the King's bout had been touched during his indisposition and that as gold is purified by fire so the King had been tried as fine t old.—As the congregation left the church, Mr W E/ Payton, Mos Ban played the Coronation March.— At night,Mr Corney based his sermon on the tsxt " And a crown izas given unto Him " (Revelation 6, v. 2.) He pointed out chat the scene in Westminster Abbey on the previous day was no doubt one of dazzling splendour but it was not to be compared in grandeur and solemnity with the scene which John described in the book of Re- velation. The preacher drew a word picture ot tb crowning of the Lamb after His redemption of tho world, and proceeded to speak of the Fatherhood of God and our Divine relation hip with Him. The choir sang the chorug The King shall rejoice" and " Hallelujah " from " Judas Maccabens." The Corona- tion hymn entitled " Corona." composed by the preacher, and set to music by Mr W H Payton, was tiny at the opening of the service and at the close the National Anthem was-sung.

AGED PEOPLE'S TREAT.

PROCEEDINGS IN THE PARR HALL.

Thursday witnessed the final stage in the Corona- tion celebrations at Warrington, when a treat was given to the aged people, dinner being provided for the men and tea for the women. At noon between 300 and 400 veterans in industrial warfare, whose age ranged from 60 to SO years of age, assembled in the Parr Hall and were provided with a substantial dinner consisting of roast beef, roast mutton, roast pork, peas and potatoes, and a moderate allowance of beer. followed by a gift of clay pipes and tobacco A grant e.f £lOO had been made from the Coronation funds for the purpose, and the arrangements were left entirely in the hands of the Old Men's Christmas Dinner and Aged Women's Tea Committee who have had ranch experience in providing treats ol this kind. Ntr and Mrs Richard Shackleton Isuperintended the preparation of the dinner and a number of ladies and gentlemen waited upon the diners. Shortly after twelve o'clock the hot joints were placed upon the tables and the Rev W Brace- camp was then called upon to say grace. "God save the King," was afterwards sung, Mr George Cropper singing the first verse as a solo. Mr Percy Randall accompanied on the organ and also played a selection of music during the progress of the dinner.

Amongst those present and who assisted to attend to the wants of the CttleStA were the Mayor and Mayorsse (Mr and Mrs J Chsrlton Parr). Lady Grestiall, the Revs William Braeeeamn, 11 R Wild, and F J Matthews, Alderman Sutton (president of the old Men's Christmas dinner and Aged women's Tea), Mr Peter Grimes (hon treasurer). Mr TA Sutton (hon secretary). Councillor Smethurst (vice- president), and the followine mgmilers of the am. mittee :—Messrs George P,lling, R ShacklPton. J Nield, Alfred Jolley, and W ForAhaw as well as Alderman Shaw-Green and Miss Shaw-Green, Coun.'

cillor Malkin, Messrs Luke Talbot (chief constable), John Harrison, George HoHord, Wilfred Skelton, George Watkins, Noah Legge, George Cropper, F H Aukland,and J Strettle, Mesdames Bracecamp, Sme- thurst, Holfold, Longdin, Pilling, Shackleton, and the Misses Pierpoint (St Austin's), Legge,Smethuret and others.

The MAYOR,who had a cordial reception,delivered a short speech at the conclusion of the dinner Addressing the assembly as old friends and neigh- bours, he said it was his pleasing duty in the fast instance to welcome them there that day. He would begin by welcoming them in the name of the King—(applause)—because he felt the committee were carrying out his Majesty's dearest wishes in giving that dinner. (Hear, hear.) He daresay they knew that when the Coronation was arranged the I King's first wish was to give a good dinner to all his poor subjects and it was to carry out those wishes that the very kind and active committee had managed to arrange it so well that day. To them, they owed a debt of gratitude and they also wished to recognies those kind sub- scribers who had helped to pay the piper. (Applause.) There was one thing which he was sure was uppermost in all their minds and that was their thankfulness to Almighty God for the restoration of the King to health, (Hear, hear). A short time ago they knew how sad all felt when the King was stricken down with illness, how anxiously they watched the bulletin day by day, and bow by almost a miracle the King had recovered his health and strength. (Applause). He saw by the papers to-day that his Majesty had been'tnaking a speech to his troops. (Applause). It was the King's wish to do his duty as a king and he (the Mayor) was sure we should have a king whom we could respect, love and admire (Applause). The Mayor proceeded to read the King's message to his people and observed that he believed every word of it came from the heart of the King himself. The King felt everything which the beautiful message expressed and they might join with the King in thanking Almighty God that He had placed him in the proud position he held to day. (Applause.) He (the Major),thought they must all recognise that they were met at an auspicious time —at a happy moment—just after celebrating the peace which had brought us so much happiness and enabled so many of our sons and gallant soldiers to come home again (Applause.) He was sure old and young would look upon the year 1902 as a year of rejoicing and a record year. We had had the Coronation under circumstances of magnificence and splendour which perhaps had never been equalled in the annals of this country and we had celebrated a peace which had brougbt glory and honour to this country and shown that England is England still, and that her sons are well able to uphold her majesty and prestige all over the world (Applause.) Alderman SUTTON, in the course of a few remarks pointed out that the Mayor had contributed very handsomely indeed to the funds necessary for pro- viding that treat in celebration of the Coronation of his Majesty King Edward VII. The Coronation Committee had voted as= of money to cover the expenses of the Old Men's Christmas Dinner and Aged Women's Tea Committee, in providing that treat. The annual Christmas treat was an institu- tion wh.ich had existed for almost half a century and it had become a very popular and successful affair. In addition to the annual treats several others had been given to the aged people. On the oceasion of the Jubilee of her late Majesty in 1887 over 700 men and women were entertained to a special tea at the Drill Hall and again at the cele- bration 'of the Diamond Jubilee of the late Queen, a sum of £lOO was voted to the committee and a dinner and tea were pro- vided with the money for nearly 1,500 poople No one now living could ever expect to take part in another Royal jubilee, for we were hoping that the King would reign over us for many years, and that his Majesty's reign would be as happy and prosperous as that of the King's noble mother—good Queen Victoria. (Applause.) The alderman pro- ceeded to point out that it was possible for some of those present to have witnessed three or four coro- nations, for he understood that there were some men nearly 90 years of age- He hoped all present would be able to attend the next Christmas dinner and that they would meet together as heartily as they did that day. (Applause.) They were honour- ed with the I,resence of the Meyoress and Lady Greenall. (Applause ) He was glad that these ladies took an interest in everything connected with the old people of Ws.rrington, and he hoped their interest would continue for many years to come. Sir Gilbert Greensll would also ;lave been present, hut another engagement altogether pre- vented him from attending
